The set difference — DOF windmills whose OAS key has no matching USWTDB faa_ors — is a geolocated, turbine-by-turbine map of the American wind fleet that does not exist yet. I measured it: 16,708 orphan windmills, 7,873 of them freshly touched (JDATE 2024-26, tip height >300 ft), 92% ACTION=A (newly added), 89% VERIFIED=U. They are not noise: they cluster into 61 coherent new-farm footprints under tight study-number blocks — e.g. 1,007 turbines all filed under one 2023WTW0xxxx block at 35.06N/-102.64W in the Texas Panhandle, 515 in SE Colorado, 455 more in the Panhandle. Decisive detail: the pipeline's mean tip height is 637 ft (194 m) against the incumbent fleet's 443 ft (135 m) — the machines being permitted are 44% taller than the ones already standing.
- **How to collect it:** Three public pulls, all confirmed live and unauthenticated during this session. (1) https://aeronav.faa.gov/Obst_Data/DAILY_DOF_CSV.zip — 20.5 MB, HTTP 200, Last-Modified 2026-08-11, one file DOF.CSV, 652,902 rows, fixed schema: OAS, VERIFIED STATUS, COUNTRY, STATE, CITY, LATDEC, LONDEC, DMSLAT, DMSLON, TYPE, QUANTITY, AGL, AMSL, LIGHTING, ACCURACY, MARKING, FAA STUDY, ACTION, JDATE. Filter TYPE='WINDMILL'. (2) https://eersc.usgs.gov/api/uswtdb/v1/turbines — PostgREST, no key, returns 75,727 built turbines with faa_ors, faa_asn, eia_id, t_hh, t_rd, t_ttlh, t_cap, p_name, p_year, xlong, ylat (use -L; a bare curl 301s). The faa_ors field is the same "SS-NNNNNN" OAS key as the DOF, so the join is exact. (3) EIA-860 Schedule 4 owner file, joined on USWTDB's eia_id, maps each operating plant to its parent company and ownership percentage — that's what converts a wake-exposed farm into a ticker. Then: cluster the orphan coordinates into farm footprints (single-linkage at ~3 km), orient each cluster against the incumbent's site-specific wind rose (ERA5 or the nearest ASOS), and run a standard Jensen/Park or Gaussian wake deficit model out to 40 km. Case-level color (sponsor name, determination date, expiration) comes from the OE/AAA portal at oeaaa.faa.gov by study number. Refresh the DOF daily and diff on ACTION/JDATE.
- **The bet / trade:** Two legs. PRIMARY (the real edge): on 5-10 year volumetric wind hedges and proxy revenue swaps, take the generation-SHORT side — be the fixed receiver, or buy wind-production puts / P90 floors referencing site output or a Speedwell wind index — on the cohort my screen flags as wake-exposed, and as a hedge provider quote the generation-LONG (fixed payer) side only on wake-protected sites. This is deal-selection alpha on a book, not a directional weather punt. Settlement horizon matches the hedge tenor, with the wake landing 12-30 months after the upwind Determination of No Hazard. SECONDARY (liquid, faster): via the USWTDB eia_id → EIA-860 owner join, short the equity/units of the yieldco or IPP with the highest fleet-weighted upwind exposure against a matched long in an unexposed peer; 4-10 quarter horizon, harvested as repeated "below long-term average wind resource" production misses. My screen currently names the exposed assets concretely: King Creek 1 (TX, 397 MW, 331 permitted turbines in its 20 km upwind sector), Azure Sky (TX, 350 MW, 314), Mariah North (TX, 230 MW, 305), Diversion Wind (TX, 201 MW, 200), Canadian Breaks (TX, 193 MW, 201), Spinning Spur 3 (TX), Ironwood I and Pratt Wind (KS), Minonk (IL), Buffalo Gap 1/2/3 and Horse Hollow III (TX), Armadillo Flats (OK), Spearville 3 (KS), NedPower Mount Storm (WV).
- **Mechanism:** Wind is a rival good and upwind turbines steal it. A turbine extracts momentum and leaves a velocity deficit that persists tens of kilometers downstream; Akhtar et al. (Scientific Reports 2021, 132 cites) measure mean wind-speed deficits extending 35-40 km downwind under prevailing southwesterlies and neighboring downwind farm capacity factors cut by 20%+ offshore, and Lundquist et al. (Nature Energy 2018, 307 cites) titled the onshore economic version of this exactly "Costs and consequences of wind turbine wake effects arising from uncoordinated wind energy development." Onshore recovery is faster than offshore, so the honest onshore number for a farm with heavy permitted build 5-20 km upwind is roughly 1-5% of annual energy production — permanent, not weather. The height asymmetry I measured makes it worse than a naive model says: at 194 m tip height the new machines' rotors sweep air well above the 135 m incumbents' rotor plane, so their wakes descend onto the older fleet from above and, sitting in less-sheared and less-turbulent flow, persist further downstream. Now the pricing error. A wind hedge is struck on a P50 capacity factor built from MERRA-2 or ERA5 reanalysis, correlated to on-site history. Reanalysis contains no wind-farm drag term whatsoever — the model physically cannot represent a turbine — and the site's own production history embeds only the wakes that already existed when it was recorded. Both inputs to the P50 are structurally blind to turbines that do not exist yet. So a hedge struck today on a site that will be surrounded by 2 GW of taller upwind machines in 2028 is written against a number that is already wrong and gets monotonically worse for the life of the trade. The DOF tells you which sites those are, by coordinate, today.
- **Who's on the other side:** Hedge providers writing the fixed-payer leg — bank commodity desks (Macquarie-type), reinsurance and ILS balance sheets (Allianz, Munich Re, Swiss Re Corporate Solutions), and specialist shops like Resurety, which advertises $1.5bn in clean energy transactions and 25 GW+ under management. Also yieldco and IPP equity analysts, and the P50 consultants themselves (DNV, UL, Everoze). None of them are stupid; they are structurally blind in a specific, durable way. An independent wake study is commissioned once, at financing, for the subject project, against neighbors that already exist. Nobody re-runs an incumbent's P50 when a stranger permits a thousand turbines upwind — because the incumbent is not a party to that FAA proceeding, receives no notice of it, and under US law has no wind-rights protection analogous to a solar access easement, so it could not block it anyway. The externality has no owner and therefore no analyst. Worse, the failure mode is perfectly camouflaged: three consecutive years of structural wake loss and three consecutive years of genuinely poor wind are indistinguishable on an earnings call, and management has a ready-made, sympathetic, entirely conventional excuse ("below long-term average wind resource") that analysts have been trained to model as mean-reverting noise. It is not noise. It never reverts.
- **Information lead:** Long and asymmetric. A turbine enters the DOF with exact coordinates at Determination of No Hazard, typically 6-18 months before ground is broken. It flips VERIFIED U→O and enters USWTDB only after satellite imagery confirms it physically exists — and USWTDB's lag is measurable in the data I pulled: its newest online year holds just 1,097 turbines against 2,625 for 2022, meaning recent build is still working through confirmation. EIA-860M is monthly, ~2 months lagged, plant-level, and carries no turbine geometry at all, so it can never support a wake calculation. Call the geolocated-build lead 12-36 months. But the deeper lead is that the P50 revision itself is never computed by anyone: the market only learns the wake happened after two or three years of realized production misses force a resource reassessment, which puts the effective lead closer to 3-5 years on the number that actually prices the hedge. The position can also be staged, which is the practical trick: screen on the Determination of No Hazard, then size up on the Form 7460-2 Part 1 "Notice of Actual Construction," which the developer must file within 5 days of commencing work, at the same coordinate precision. That filing converts a permit option into a certainty months before any commercial database registers the project as under construction.
- **Why still under-exploited:** The DOF is an aviation-safety artifact. Its consumers are aeronautical chart producers, helicopter EMS route planners, and drone operators — not energy analysts. It ships as a fixed-schema flat CSV inside a zip on an FAA aeronav server, with an obstacle-accuracy code where you would want a project name and no notion whatsoever of ownership, capacity, or commissioning. USWTDB is used by energy people but is deliberately, explicitly backward-looking: imagery-verified turbines only. The alpha lives precisely in the seam between them, and extracting it is genuinely manual work — parse the aviation file, key OAS to faa_ors, cluster tens of thousands of orphan coordinates into plausible farm footprints, disambiguate them by FAA study-number block, orient each against a site-specific wind rose, then run an actual wake model. Nothing sold commercially does this. Enverus, Wood Mackenzie and Cleanview track projects, queues and megawatts; none of them carry turbine-level geometry for unbuilt assets, and none compute cross-owner wake externalities, because there is no natural customer: the developer causing the wake has no reason to quantify it and the incumbent suffering it has no legal remedy. The wake consultancies sell per-project studies at financing, one asset at a time, forward-looking only for their own client. A continuously-refreshed national ledger of who is about to have their wind stolen, and by exactly which permitted turbines, does not exist as a product. The one genuinely public, genuinely predictive field in the whole chain — VERIFIED STATUS = "U" — is documented as an aviation survey flag and read by nobody as a construction-pipeline signal.
- **Rough +EV:** Screen output, computed rather than guessed: of 150,511 MW across 1,796 US operating projects ≥50 MW, 69 projects totaling 12,188 MW (8.1% of the screened fleet) have ≥25 permitted-but-unbuilt turbines inside a 20 km upwind sector. At 1.5/3/5% AEP wake loss that is 0.58/1.15/1.92 TWh/yr of production the market has not priced, worth roughly $20M/$40M/$67M per year at $35/MWh. Per deal: on a 10-year, 200 MW proxy revenue swap, a 3% volume error is ~$0.66M/yr, ~$5-6M NPV — larger than the hedge provider's entire margin on the trade, which is why deal selection rather than deal direction is where the money is. Realistically only ~3-5 GW of that 12 GW sits behind a tradable hedge or a listed owner. Firing rate: roughly 15-30 new incumbent-exposure events per year as DNH blocks land, each a multi-year hold; on the equity leg expect 2-4 exploitable owners at any time, worth perhaps 3-8% relative underperformance versus a matched peer over 4-8 quarters. I'd put the equity pair leg at Sharpe 0.5-0.8 — quarter-to-quarter weather swamps the structural signal — and the hedge-selection leg materially better, because refusing to write a mispriced deal carries no variance at all.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The biggest uncertainty by a wide margin is the DNH-to-steel conversion rate. A Determination of No Hazard is a permit, not a commitment: developers deliberately over-file turbine positions to preserve micrositing optionality, so a 1,007-position filing may become a 400-turbine farm, and ERCOT/SPP interconnection-queue attrition kills whole projects late. If only 25-40% of my 7,873 orphan coordinates ever get built, the true P50 shift is a fraction of the modeled one and my inverse-distance 20 km weighting over-attributes badly. Two mitigants: stage the position — screen on DNH, size only on the Form 7460-2 Part 1 construction-start filing, which is a fact rather than an option and still leads every commercial database by months; and calibrate the conversion rate empirically by backtesting historical DOF vintages against what USWTDB later confirmed as built, which is a clean, fully public backtest available today. Secondary uncertainty: the headline 20%+ capacity-factor hit is offshore, where low surface roughness lets deficits persist; onshore turbulent mixing recovers the wake faster, so 1-3% is the defensible planning number and 5% should be treated as a tail case, not a base case. Third: some of the 16,708 orphans are USWTDB match failures or decommissioned units rather than pipeline, though the 92% ACTION=A / 89% VERIFIED=U split and the tight study-block clustering argue that contamination is modest.

---
## 42. The Barn Ledger

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Carbon markets & environmental credits. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** CARB's LCFS fuel-pathway machinery, two artifacts nobody joins: (1) the certified-pathway ledger `current-pathways_all.xlsx` — verified live, 3,756 rows, "Last Updated 2026-08-07", refreshed ~weekly, columns include Class (Tier 1/2), Facility Location, Feedstock, Fuel Type, **Current Certified CI**, **Certification Date**, Company (ID), Facility (ID); and (2) the annual "LCFS Pathways Requiring Public Comments" pages (2015–2026), where each pending Tier 2 application posts a full package PDF. The cover sheet carries four dates the market never otherwise sees: Deemed Complete Date, Posted for Comment Date, CI Certified Date, CI Start Date (verified on app B0901: complete 5/20/26, posted 7/17/26, certified TBD). The package also contains the CARB staff summary, the CA-GREET 3.0 lifecycle inputs, **the facility's air permits** (which give throughput), and references to the herd-count / manure-fraction calculations that set the CI. The buried variable is the CI column. For livestock-manure pathways it is not just negative, it is wildly dispersed: 721 pathways, median −297, min −1,887, max −68 gCO2e/MJ — a 25x spread within one feedstock. That single number is the dominant term in both aggregate LCFS credit supply and every RNG project's unit economics, and it sits in an unlinked .xlsx behind a legacy `/classic/` path.
- **How to collect it:** Plain HTTPS, no auth, no login wall, no ToS restriction — a California public agency publishing regulatory records. (a) `curl https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/sites/default/files/classic/fuels/lcfs/fuelpathways/current-pathways_all.xlsx` (~1.7 MB); parse sheet "All Pathways", header on row 4; snapshot and diff daily — new rows = newly certified pathways, and cell B3 stamps the refresh date. (b) Scrape `https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/resources/documents/lcfs-pathways-requiring-public-comments` and its year-archive siblings (`/2025-…` back to `/2015-…`) for `Application No. Bxxxx` rows with posted date, comment deadline, and comment/response links; pull each cover PDF at `…/fuelpathways/comments/tier2/b0901_cover.pdf` for the four-date lifecycle, and the full package for the air permit (throughput in MMBtu/yr or scfm) and the CA-GREET inputs. (c) Build the join CARB never publishes: Company (ID) → corporate parent (public vs. private), Facility (ID) → EPA RFS registration and state air-permit throughput → implied MMBtu → credits = (benchmark CI − pathway CI) × MJ × EER / 1e6. Two people, a week, then a cron job. I verified the whole chain end to end: downloaded the xlsx, parsed 3,756 rows, parsed the B0901 cover PDF, and computed the posted→certified lag distribution across 36 matched applications.
- **The bet / trade:** Primary: SHORT California LCFS credits — OPIS-assessed LCFS credit forwards / OTC brokered credit swaps (cleared LCFS swaps where available), 2–4 quarter horizon, entered now and rolled quarterly, exiting on the CARB quarterly data print that confirms the bank build. The signal is directional-conditional, and it is currently screaming supply: livestock-manure pathway certifications went 180 (2024) → 41 (all of 2025, while the amended LCFS sat rejected at OAL and CARB effectively froze) → **188 in 2026 through 7 August alone**, median CI −264, tail −1,887. That is a backlog of the single most credit-dense fuel in the program clearing all at once. Secondary, easier to execute for a small book: an equity pair inside the renewable-fuels complex, long/short on certification-flow acceleration, held 1–2 quarters through the earnings print. Verified 2025+ certification counts by issuer: Phillips 66 51, Darling/Diamond Green 30, Calumet–Montana Renewables 21, Valero 21, Aemetis 8, Clean Energy Fuels 7, Gevo 6, Montauk 1.
- **Mechanism:** An LCFS credit is minted as (benchmark CI − pathway CI) × energy × EER. Certification is the *last* regulatory gate: the day CARB posts a certified CI, that facility can begin monetizing. Do the arithmetic on a dairy pathway at CI −300 against a 2026 diesel benchmark near +85: 385 gCO2e/MJ × 1,055 MJ/MMBtu = 0.41 credits per MMBtu ≈ $28/MMBtu of LCFS revenue at $70/credit — roughly eight times the commodity value of the gas itself. At the −800 tail it is ~$65/MMBtu. So (i) in aggregate, the CI-weighted pace of new certifications *is* forward LCFS credit supply, and the credit bank is what sets the credit price; (ii) at single-name level, the facility-specific CI is the revenue multiplier, and it varies 25x within the same feedstock, so no analyst can shortcut it with a category average. The 2026 flush is mechanically explainable: the 2024 amendments were rejected by the Office of Administrative Law in early 2025, certifications stalled, the amendments took effect 1 July 2025, and CARB is now clearing the queue. Those credits are being generated today and will not surface in CARB's published credit-generation data until roughly mid-2027.
- **Who's on the other side:** Obligated parties — Chevron, Valero, Marathon, PBF, Phillips 66 — buy LCFS credits on a compliance calendar, quarterly and annually, essentially price-insensitively; they are hedging a regulatory liability, not expressing a supply view. The speculative side (Vitol, Freepoint, Hartree, a handful of specialist funds) prices off the last CARB quarterly data summary, which is ~4–5 months stale, plus OPIS/Argus assessments that are by construction backward-looking. There is no CFTC Commitments of Traders report for LCFS credits, no exchange tape for spot, and no positioning data of any kind — the market's only fundamental anchor is a lagged government spreadsheet. On the equity leg, sell-side energy analysts model RNG and renewable-diesel revenue from company guidance and generic CI assumptions; not one of them is joining a 3,756-row .xlsx to facility ownership. They are slow because the work is unglamorous PDF-and-Excel plumbing across twelve years of legacy CARB pages, and because the payoff variable (CI) looks like a compliance detail rather than an eight-bagger revenue multiplier.
- **Information lead:** Three separate leads, all measured, not assumed. (1) Versus CARB's own quarterly LCFS data — the market's reference for the credit bank — **6 to 12 months**: certification → credit generation → quarterly publication at ~4–5 months' lag. (2) Versus issuer disclosure: **1–2 quarters**, from certification date to the earnings call where that facility's LCFS revenue first appears. (3) Versus the certified-CI table itself, for pathways sitting in the public-comment queue: I matched 36 applications between the comment pages and the certified ledger and measured posted-for-comment → certified at **median 19 days, p25 16, p75 24, range 15–44**. So on any given pending application you know the exact CI two to six weeks before it lands in the file everyone else eventually reads — and the cover PDF's "Deemed Complete Date" (B0901: 5/20/26, posted 7/17/26) adds another ~two months of visibility into the pipeline behind it.
- **Why still under-exploited:** OPIS, Argus and Stillwater report pathway certifications *qualitatively* — "X certified a dairy pathway in Idaho" — as trade-press color. Nobody publishes the thing that matters: a CI-weighted forward credit-supply curve built by joining the full ledger to facility ownership and permitted throughput. The reason is friction, not secrecy. The ledger is a single unlinked .xlsx on a legacy `/classic/` path with a merged-cell header on row 4; the pending queue is twelve years of separate annual pages, each holding dozens of application cover PDFs; and ownership is encoded as opaque CARB Company/Facility IDs ("Chevron Sobek RNG LLC – JM Larson (F00677)") that must be hand-mapped to parents. The economically decisive field has 25x dispersion inside one feedstock, so there is no averaging shortcut — you have to do the facility-by-facility work. Meanwhile the whole complex is covered as an *energy* story by people who don't read air-quality filings, and as an *environmental* story by people who don't trade. Tellingly, the only parties reliably reading these application packages are advocacy groups: the Animal Legal Defense Fund has filed opposition comments on the pending Chevron dairy applications B0901, B0897 and on B0858 — and those oppositions are themselves a measurable delay signal (B0858 took 44 days posted→certified against a 19-day median), which nobody is pricing either.
- **Rough +EV:** Modest but real, and it compounds across two legs. LCFS credits carry roughly 40–60% annualized vol with 10–15% typical quarterly moves and effectively zero public positioning data. The aggregate signal fires cleanly maybe 4–8 times a year (quarterly rebalance plus 2–4 sharp certification-flush or -drought episodes); when CI-weighted certification flow is more than ~1.5σ from trend — as it is right now at 4.6x the prior year — I'd expect roughly 55–62% directional accuracy on the next 3-month move, i.e. an expected edge of ~2–4% of notional per quarter, call it 8–15% annualized on deployed capital before costs, in a market where bid/ask on brokered credit forwards is wide (1–3%) and eats a real slice of that. The single-name leg is lumpier and better: 2–5 events a year where a specific public issuer's facility certifies at a CI far off consensus, worth a 3–8% single-name move over 1–2 quarters, at maybe 60–65% hit rate because the disclosure is unambiguous rather than statistical. Capacity is the binding constraint — this is a few tens of millions of dollars of edge, not hundreds.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Every factual link is verified — I downloaded and parsed the ledger, parsed a live application cover PDF, and measured the 19-day posted→certified lag across 36 matched applications — so the data source and the lead are not in question. The biggest uncertainty is that **LCFS credit prices are policy-dominated, not supply-dominated**: CARB's scheduled benchmark step-downs, the Automatic Acceleration Mechanism (which mechanically tightens benchmarks when the bank grows and therefore works directly against the short), and OAL/litigation risk can all swamp a supply signal that is directionally correct. The second real weakness is that the ledger gives CI but not volume — the supply nowcast needs an assumed MMBtu per facility. That is recoverable, since the application packages contain the facility air permits with throughput, but it is manual work and an error bar the model has to carry honestly. If I had to name the single thing that kills this trade: the AAM firing and turning a bearish supply flush into a bullish benchmark shock.

## 43. The Millage Retreat

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Electricity capacity markets & ancillary services. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** County/state **property tax assessment appeal dockets filed by power plant owners** — the sworn, public legal record in which a generator's owner argues its own plant is worth a fraction of its assessed value. The filings contain the owner's income-approach appraisal: their internal forward capacity-price deck, forward capacity factor, and assumed remaining useful life. This is the owner's private retirement decision, made public under oath, 1–3 years before any RTO deactivation notice. Cross-checked against MSRB EMMA continuing-disclosure filings from the host school district/county, whose "principal taxpayers" tables and material-event notices flag the appeal and the anticipated revenue loss.
- **How to collect it:** All free, public, sanctioned portals; the work is the fragmentation, not the access. Verified live: (1) **Michigan Tax Tribunal Docket Lookup** — https://taxdocketlookup.apps.lara.state.mi.us/Search.aspx — searchable by Petitioner/Respondent name and by any of 83 counties; the site states filed documents are public records viewable there. Query owner entities (DTE Electric, Consumers Energy, Vistra subsidiaries). (2) **Pennsylvania UJS Portal** — https://ujsportal.pacourts.us/CaseSearch — free Common Pleas civil docket search by participant name, county, date filed, and case category; PA assessment appeals are filed as Common Pleas civil actions. (3) **Massachusetts Appellate Tax Board** — mass.gov/orgs/appellate-tax-board publishes Findings of Fact and Reports 2000–2024. (4) Ohio county Boards of Revision + Ohio Board of Tax Appeals docket; (5) Illinois PTAB (ptab.illinois.gov); (6) NJ Tax Court via NJ eCourts; (7) NY RPTL Article 7 tax certiorari petitions via NYSCEF (iapps.courts.state.ny.us/nyscef); (8) Maryland Tax Court; (9) Indiana Board of Tax Review. Join key: EIA-860 plant lat/long → county parcel viewer → parcel ID and owner LLC name (owner LLCs also recoverable from FERC market-based-rate filings), then run that LLC string against each docket. Sweep on the statutory calendar: OH ~Mar 31, NJ Apr 1, MI commercial/industrial May 31, NY grievance day 4th Tue of May then petition by Jul 31, PA Aug 1/Sep 1 by county. Also pull the raw assessed-value time series off county parcel viewers as a zero-effort proxy, and set EMMA alerts on the host school district's CUSIPs.
- **The bet / trade:** Two linked legs. **Primary:** into a scheduled PJM Base Residual Auction results date (PJM posts results on a published calendar — the 2028/29 BRA results posted July 14, 2026; the next BRA follows on PJM's return to a 3-year-forward cadence), take a directional position in the PJM/MISO-levered IPP basket — Vistra (VST), Talen (TLN), NRG, Constellation (CEG) — via 1–3 month call spreads struck around the results date. Long when the appeal flow shows an accelerating retirement pipeline in a constrained LDA (BGE, PEPCO, DPL-South, EMAAC, ComEd); fade/flatten when appeals are settling upward or counties are granting PILOT relief that keeps marginal units alive. Overlay with long ICE PJM Western Hub calendar-strip peak futures for the affected delivery years. **Secondary, single-name, 12–24 month hold:** a plant in a load pocket whose owner has filed a >50% claimed writedown is a Reliability-Must-Run candidate — RMR cost-of-service deals have been worth $100–200M/yr to a single owner (Brandon Shores/Wagner). Go long that owner's equity on the appeal filing, well before the deactivation notice that triggers the reliability study.
- **Mechanism:** A large thermal plant is usually the single largest taxpayer in its township. The first institutional act of an owner who has internally written a unit off is not a press release and not an RTO filing — it is a tax appeal, because carrying an $800M assessment on an asset you have decided is worth $80M is an immediate, quantifiable cash bleed that the tax department is mandated to stop. That appeal must be filed on a statutory annual deadline, so it happens on the *next* deadline after the internal decision, not when the owner is ready to tell the market. To win it, the owner must submit a sworn income-approach appraisal — which necessarily discloses their forward capacity price curve, dispatch assumptions, and remaining useful life. Those retirements then propagate mechanically: fewer offered UCAP megawatts into the RPM auction moves clearing price up a steep VRR demand curve, and PJM's clearing price is roughly a pure profit passthrough to merchant IPPs, whose equities move 5–15% in a single session on results. Locationally, the same retirements thin the pool of synchronized units in a reserve sub-zone, firming reserve and regulation prices there. The reverse signal is equally tradable: an appeal settled *upward*, withdrawn, or resolved into a generous PILOT is a retirement that will not happen — supply that models built off announced closures are wrongly removing.
- **Who's on the other side:** Generalist and utility-sector equity investors who mark capacity value off the last cleared auction and a sell-side retirement table. Merchant power desks and consultants whose retirement pipelines are sourced from the RTO's deactivation page, EIA-860M's "planned retirement date" field, and S&P Global — every one of which only updates *after* the owner formally notifies, which is the last step, not the first. PJM's own supply forecasts and the Independent Market Monitor's pre-auction commentary are built on formal notices only, so the official consensus is structurally lagging by construction. Nobody on a power desk has "read the county board of revision docket" in their job description; the people who do read those dockets are municipal-finance analysts and property tax attorneys, who have no view on capacity markets and no way to trade it. The information sits in the gap between two professions.
- **Information lead:** **1–3 years** over the RTO deactivation notice (PJM's minimum is 90 days; the public deactivation process typically runs 6–12 months). **1–2 years** over EIA-860M flipping a unit's planned-retirement flag. **6–18 months** over the owner's own press release, since companies sit on closure news until labor, state relations, and RMR negotiations are staged. Within a given year the signal arrives in predictable batches on statutory filing deadlines, so a monitored sweep captures it within days of filing. Against the specific catalyst, the lead is the distance from filing to the next BRA results date — typically two to six quarters, ample time to build the position cheaply before the event.
- **Why still under-exploited:** There is no vendor for this and the structural reasons are durable. The data is scattered across ~13 states and several hundred counties on a dozen incompatible portals (Michigan Tax Tribunal, PA UJS, Ohio BOR/BTA, Illinois PTAB, NJ eCourts, NYSCEF, Maryland Tax Court, Indiana BTR, Massachusetts ATB), most with no bulk export and many with scanned PDFs. The join is the real barrier: you must go EIA plant ID → lat/long → county parcel viewer → parcel number → the specific single-purpose LLC name on the deed, because plants are never titled under the parent ticker. Energy data vendors (Velocity Suite, S&P, Yes Energy) carry no county tax dockets; legal-data vendors (Lex Machina, Bloomberg Law) cover federal and appellate courts but not county boards of revision, and have no industry tagging that would surface "power plant." The dataset is filed under municipal finance, not energy, which is exactly why it has never been productized — it does not look like an energy dataset to anyone shopping for one. The universe is also small enough for a determined team: roughly 250–350 fossil and nuclear units above 200 MW across PJM and MISO, concentrated in perhaps 400 counties, with an annual filing calendar.
- **Rough +EV:** Signal frequency: roughly 15–40 material generator assessment appeals per year across the PJM/MISO footprint, of which perhaps 8–15 carry a claimed writedown large enough (>50%) to be a genuine retirement tell. Estimated conditional hit rate on deactivation-or-mothball within three years: ~50–65%, versus a ~10–15% unconditional base rate for that unit population — a real 4–5x lift on the conditional. Translating to the trade: PJM's VRR curve is steep near the reliability requirement (in the 2025/26 BRA, single-digit GW of supply change carried price from $28.92 to $269.92/MW-day), so handicapping total offered supply by ±1–2 GW out of ~135 GW ahead of the print is genuinely price-relevant. IPP single-session moves on BRA results have run 5–15%. Realistic expectation: 2–4 actionable directional calls per year, expected edge of roughly 100–250 bps per event on a levered IPP basket and materially more expressed through short-dated calls, giving low-to-mid single-digit percent annualized alpha on the sleeve — lumpy, event-clustered, not a smooth stream. The single-name RMR leg is rarer (perhaps one setup every 12–18 months) but is the fat tail: correctly front-running an RMR award on a mid-cap IPP is a 10–25% single-name move. Cost side is essentially labor: one analyst-quarter to build the parcel-to-plant crosswalk, then a few days per statutory sweep window.
- **Confidence:** **Medium.** The data source is verified real and freely accessible (I confirmed the Michigan Tax Tribunal docket lookup searches by petitioner and county with documents public, the PA UJS portal offers free Common Pleas civil search by participant name and county, and the Massachusetts ATB publishes decisions), the plant-owner-files-appeal-before-closing pattern is well attested historically (Brayton Point/Somerset, Homer City, the upstate NY coal fleet), and the trade vehicle is clean and liquid. The biggest uncertainty is **false positives from routine protective appeals**: large owners file assessment challenges on some cadence regardless of retirement intent, so the docket index alone is close to noise — the edge lives entirely in the *magnitude and methodology of the claimed writedown*, which means actually pulling and reading the appraisal exhibits rather than counting filings. Two secondary risks compound it: capacity prices at $325–450/MW-day can reverse a retirement decision that was made when prices were $30, breaking the appeal→closure link precisely in the regime where the trade matters most; and PJM's clearing price is now partly administratively bounded (the Pennsylvania collar), which caps the payoff even when the supply call is right. I would want one back-tested cycle of hand-read appraisals before sizing this beyond a research position.

---
## 44. The LIQFLARE Ledger

**Arena:** financial-market (NYMEX/ICE natural gas futures, options, and Gulf Coast basis swaps)
**Domain:** Natural gas storage & LNG. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** TCEQ Air Emission Event Report Database (the "EER" database) — Texas's mandatory upset/flaring disclosure system under 30 TAC Ch. 101 Subchapter F (§101.201 emissions events, §101.211 scheduled maintenance/startup/shutdown). Every Texas-sited LNG liquefaction plant must file an INITIAL report within ~24 hours of any reportable flaring/venting event, and the public record carries a free-text root-cause narrative, the specific TRAINS affected, the emission point (e.g. EPN "LIQFLARE"), event start/end datetimes, computed Event Duration, and an itemized contaminant table in pounds. Verified live this session: Freeport LNG Liquefaction Plant (RN103196689), Freeport LNG Pretreatment Facility (RN106481500, a SEPARATE regulated entity), Corpus Christi Liquefaction LLC (CN604136374, 73 records), Golden Pass LNG Terminal LLC (CN603161019). Record counts at LNG-named entities: 263 EMISSIONS EVENT, 44 AIR STARTUP, 4 AIR SHUTDOWN, 1 MAINTENANCE. Sample record 444109 reads verbatim: "During operation of Freeport LNG's Liquefaction Plant, Trains 1, 2, and 3 tripped due to an incoming power feed interruption. The trips resulted in unplanned and unavoidable venting to the Liquefaction Flare (EPN: LIQFLARE)" — with Event Duration: 17 hours, 32 minutes. That is a train-level, cause-level, duration-level disclosure that exists nowhere in pipeline data.
- **How to collect it:** All free, no login, no API key. (1) LIVE TRIGGER: poll https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/RSSDataFeed.xml every 10-15 min. It is a rolling 7-day window regenerated continuously (observed lastBuildDate Thu 13 Aug 2026 19:46 CST with newest item start Wed 12 Aug 17:30 => ~26h observed posting latency). Each item is "<incident#> - <RN> <FACILITY NAME>". (2) WATCHLIST MATCH on RN103196689, RN106481500, CN604136374, CN603161019, plus new RNs for Port Arthur LNG as it commissions. (3) DETAIL PULL: GET https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/index.cfm?fuseaction=main.getDetails&target=<incident#> and parse Report Type (INITIAL/FINAL), Report Date, Incident Status, Event/Activity Type, "Date and Time Event Discovered or Scheduled Activity Start", "...Ended", Event Duration, "Process Unit or Area Common Names", Facility Common Name + FIN (e.g. "Liquefaction Trains 1, 2, & 3" / FIN TRAINS123), Emission Point Number, the contaminant table (lbs), and the field "Cause of Emission Event or Excess Opacity Event, or Reason for Scheduled Activity". (4) BACKTEST/BACKFILL via the undocumented GET search API: index.cfm?fuseaction=main.searchresults&newsearch=yes&re_name=LNG&usefullwildcard=yes&ls_event_typ_cd=AIRU&event_start_beg_dt=MM/DD/YYYY. Event type codes are AIRU (emissions event), AIRM (maintenance), AIRSTARTUP, AIRSHUTDWN, AIROPACITY. Results pages also expose an Excel export with county/region. (5) Join RN -> terminal -> train -> Bcf/d nameplate by hand (one-time ~200 rows), and pair with free pipeline feedgas nominations for the volume leg. Stack is one cron job plus a regex parser; the site is a 2000s-era ColdFusion app, so scraping is trivial but ugly.
- **The bet / trade:** Conditional, event-driven trade on NYMEX Henry Hub natural gas (NG) prompt-month futures and short-dated/weekly NG options, held 2-10 trading days. When a Texas liquefaction emissions-event filing hits: (A) if the cause narrative is electrical/instrument/utility-side ("incoming power feed interruption", "loss of instrument air", "lightning", "utility voltage sag") AND the event already carries a closed end-time or sub-48h duration -> FADE the feedgas-driven selloff: buy prompt NG futures or sell prompt puts, target the reversal within 2-5 sessions. (B) if the cause names rotating equipment, refrigerant/propane compressor, cold box, or the PRETREATMENT facility RN (amine/dehydration — constrains ALL trains, not one) -> PRESS the move: short prompt NG / buy prompt puts, 5-10 sessions, because the market's default 2-3 day outage assumption is far too optimistic. Sizing scales with (trains named / total trains) x nameplate Bcf/d. Secondary and higher-signal-to-noise: Houston Ship Channel basis swaps for Freeport/Golden Pass events and Agua Dulce / South Texas basis for Corpus Christi events — the local supply-push is cleaner than the Henry Hub move. Separate slower trade: AIR STARTUP filings at Golden Pass / Port Arthur are a commissioning tell — go long the deferred NG strip / long winter-summer spreads on confirmed train light-off, multi-week horizon.
- **Mechanism:** A US liquefaction train that trips stops consuming feedgas. Texas liquefaction is roughly 5-9 Bcf/d and rising (Freeport ~2.1, Corpus Christi ~2.5+ with Stage 3, Golden Pass ~2.6 ramping, Port Arthur behind it) against ~105 Bcf/d US demand, so a multi-train trip is a 2-4% instantaneous demand shock — enough to move prompt NG 1.5-4% in a session. The market sees the VOLUME drop immediately in pipeline feedgas nominations. What it cannot see is DURATION, and duration is what determines whether the price move is a one-day blip or a multi-week repricing that also drags the storage-injection path and the next several EIA weekly reports. Physically, a train tripped by a power-feed interruption or instrument-air loss is a restart-sequence problem measured in hours (record 444109: 17h32m). A train tripped by a refrigerant compressor seal, a cold box, or an amine unit in the separately-permitted pretreatment facility is a parts-and-inspection problem measured in weeks — and a pretreatment outage bottlenecks every downstream train at once regardless of how healthy the liquefaction trains are. Texas air law forces the operator to state which of these it was, in plain English, in a public database, within ~24 hours — because flaring the plant inventory is itself a reportable emissions event and the flare tonnage scales with how much hydrocarbon had to be blown down. Bigger blowdown = deeper de-inventory = longer restart. So the flare ledger is simultaneously a cause disclosure, a scope disclosure (which trains / which flare / which RN), and a severity gauge (pounds flared), all of which map to outage duration, which maps to the correct size of the gas price move.
- **Who's on the other side:** Three groups. (1) Systematic/CTA and retail flow in NG, UNG and BOIL reacting to the headline "Freeport feedgas collapses" print — they trade the volume, have no duration view, and mechanically overshoot on short trips and undershoot on mechanical failures. (2) The gas analytics vendor stack — Criterion Research, Wood Mackenzie, LSEG, East Daley, Celsius Energy — whose entire data lineage is FERC/NAESB pipeline electronic bulletin board nominations and scheduled quantities. Those products are excellent at "how many Bcf/d moved" and structurally silent on "why and for how long," because that information simply is not in a nomination record. Their clients inherit the same blind spot. (3) Energy journalists, who publish "the cause was not immediately clear; the company did not respond to a request for comment" — a sentence that is routinely false in the sense that the cause IS already public in a Texas state database. The people who genuinely know duration in real time — the terminal operator, its EPC contractor, its offtakers — are either hedgers rather than speculators or are constrained from trading on it, so their knowledge does not reach the screen quickly. The result is a repeating pattern where the first-day price move is essentially an uninformed guess about duration and gets substantially revised within a week.
- **Information lead:** Honest accounting, because this is where most versions of this idea oversell. On the FACT of an outage there is NO lead — intraday pipeline nomination cycles reveal the feedgas drop at least as fast, sometimes faster. The lead is entirely on CAUSE, SCOPE and DURATION: roughly 12-36 hours ahead of the market, and often 3-7 days ahead, because the market otherwise learns duration only by watching whether feedgas recovers day after day, or by waiting for a company statement that frequently never comes for non-material events. Observed posting latency on the RSS feed was ~26 hours from event start to public visibility, against a market that typically does not resolve the duration question for 3-10 days. Two channels carry a genuine forward lead: scheduled MAINTENANCE/shutdown filings, where I verified an INITIAL report filed 06/23/2026 for an activity starting 07/01/2026 — 8 days of advance notice, ahead of any pipeline maintenance posting (note: only 1 AIRM record exists at LNG-named entities, so this channel is real but thin for LNG specifically); and AIR STARTUP filings, where the Freeport post-explosion restart shows the PRETREATMENT facility startup beginning 12/15/2022 and the LIQUEFACTION plant startup not beginning until 02/03/2023 — the pretreatment RN led the liquefaction RN by roughly seven weeks, and pretreatment must run before liquefaction can. That RN-ordering relationship is a genuine multi-week leading indicator of a terminal restart that no feedgas series can produce, because feedgas stays near zero throughout.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Mining TCEQ emission events is a semi-known trick — but it is known on the REFINED PRODUCTS and petrochemical desks, where people watch Baytown, Motiva and Deer Park for crack-spread trades. It has essentially not been ported to natural gas desks, because gas analytics grew up on a completely different data lineage (FERC/NAESB pipeline EBBs) and nobody's product joins Texas air-permit registration numbers to LNG train nameplate capacity. The specific work that creates the edge is unglamorous and manual: mapping RN -> terminal -> train FIN -> Bcf/d; knowing that Freeport's pretreatment facility is a separate RN from its liquefaction plant and that a pretreatment event is far more bearish than a same-sized liquefaction event; and classifying free-text cause narratives into short-restart versus long-repair buckets, which is a small NLP/taxonomy problem over a few hundred historical records. On top of that, the dataset is deliberately unpleasant: a 2000s ColdFusion app with no documented API, no bulk download, an undocumented GET search interface, and a 7-day RSS window that forces you to run continuous collection to build history. The killer for a commercial vendor is geographic fragmentation — Sabine Pass, Cameron, Calcasieu Pass and Plaquemines are LOUISIANA and appear nowhere in TCEQ, so a "complete" US LNG outage product would need LDEQ, Louisiana State Police hazmat notifications and the National Response Center bolted on separately with different schemas. That incompleteness is exactly why no vendor has productized it and why a small team willing to run a partial-coverage signal on the Texas half — which is the fastest-growing half, with Golden Pass and Port Arthur coming — can still get paid for it.
- **Rough +EV:** Frequency: Freeport alone logged 15 emissions events in the first ~7.5 months of 2026 (an annualized ~24); Corpus Christi has 73 lifetime records; Golden Pass is ramping and will add more. Call it ~35-50 Texas LNG emissions-event filings per year, of which realistically 10-15 are material enough (multi-train, or pretreatment, or >0.5 Bcf/d feedgas impact) to have moved prompt NG by more than ~1.5%. Edge per fire: the initial move is a duration guess; classifying it correctly within 24h should be right maybe 60-70% of the time given how legible the narratives are ("incoming power feed interruption" is not ambiguous). On a typical 1.5-4% event move, capturing 0.8-2.0% of notional at ~63% hit rate and a roughly 1:1 payoff gives ~+0.35-0.6% expected per fire before costs, times 10-15 fires = ~5-8% annual return on the capital genuinely deployed against this signal, which is not the whole book. In dollars, a disciplined $3-5mm risk allocation implies roughly $150k-450k/yr. NG futures are deep and cheap so slippage is minor; the basis-swap leg is where costs bite. Add the Golden Pass/Port Arthur AIR STARTUP commissioning channel, which is lower frequency (a handful of light-offs) but far larger per event, since 2026-27 US balance forecasts hinge on exactly when those trains actually consume gas — a correct multi-week call there is worth more than a year of trip-fading. The whole thing is meaningfully uncorrelated with the weather and directional-gas factors that dominate every other gas strategy, which is worth more than the raw Sharpe suggests.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The data source is verified beyond doubt — I pulled live records, confirmed the RN/CN identifiers, read an actual train-level cause narrative, confirmed the event-type taxonomy, and confirmed one scheduled filing landing 8 days before its activity. The biggest single uncertainty is POSTING LATENCY DISPERSION: I observed ~26 hours on one sample from the RSS feed, but I did not establish the distribution. If TCEQ's public database surfaces INITIAL reports promptly and consistently, the live edge is real; if a meaningful share of records only appear after staff review or arrive as FINAL reports weeks later (as the Freeport AIR STARTUP records did, filed 04/14/2023 for activity starting 12/15/2022), then the live channel degrades badly and the source collapses into a backtesting/duration-model input rather than a tradeable real-time signal. This is cheap to resolve: run the RSS collector for 60 days against the observed feedgas nomination timeline and measure filing-time minus event-time per record. Second-order risks: the sophisticated feedgas watchers may already infer duration reasonably well from the SHAPE of a nomination cut (a full-plant cut versus a one-third cut is visible), which would compress the edge; and Louisiana's roughly half of US liquefaction is invisible in this dataset, so on any given event the signal may simply not fire. I would fund the 60-day latency study before committing risk capital.

---
## 45. The TC36000 Docket

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Refined products & crack spreads. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** FAA Obstruction Evaluation / Airport Airspace Analysis (OE/AAA) Part 77 case files — specifically Form 7460-1 "Notice of Proposed Construction or Alteration" filings with STRUCTURE TYPE = CRANE$MOBILE / CRANE$CRAWLER, geofenced to refinery fence lines. Under 14 CFR 77.9, temporary construction equipment above 200 ft AGL (or lower inside the 100:1 imaginary surface near an airport, which covers nearly every Gulf Coast and Great Lakes refinery) requires FAA notice. Refinery turnarounds need crawler cranes with 200-500 ft booms to pull coke drums, FCC regenerator internals, reactor heads, and column sections — so the crane vendor files a public federal notice weeks-to-months before the unit comes down. The record carries LATITUDE/LONGITUDE to 8 decimals, STRUCTURE NAME (which is usually the literal crane model, e.g. "Versacrane TC36000", "Demag CC6800", "Manitowoc 2250 at Crude"), LOCATION DESCRIPTION ("West Side of Crude Unit within MPC Refinery", "XOM Coker", "Valero Houston Refinery FCC Unit"), AGL HEIGHT PROPOSED, DURATION / DURATION MONTHS, WORK SCHEDULE BEGINNING DATE, WORK SCHEDULE ENDING DATE, SPONSOR NAME, ENTERED DATE, and PRIOR ASN (the revision chain that reveals schedule slippage).
- **How to collect it:** All three access paths verified live. (1) BULK CSV, 44 columns: https://oeaaa.faa.gov/oeaaa/oe3a-external-api/downloadArchives.do?fname=Part77{REGION}{YEAR}List.gzip with REGION in {ASW, ASO, AGL, AEA, ANM, AWP, ANE, AAL} — ASW covers TX/LA/OK/AR/NM (all of PADD 3), AGL covers the Midwest. Despite the .gzip name the payload is plain UTF-8 CSV; I pulled Part77ASW2026List (12.9 MB, 13,739 rows), ASW2025 (17,929), ASW2024 (18,494), AGL2026 (20,689). FAA regenerates these weekly on Saturday evenings. (2) PUBLIC REST API for daily refresh, no key, no auth: https://oeaaa.faa.gov/oeaaa/services/ with endpoints caseList/circ/{state}, caseList/date/{type}?dateEnteredStart=&dateEnteredEnd=&state=, asnList/{type}/{year}, case/OE/{asn}, returning XML with lat/long, structureType, sponsor, aglStructureHeight, dateEntered. WADL published at https://oeaaa.faa.gov/oeaaa/external/content/Public%20WADL.xml; developer guide at /oeaaa/downloads/usermanuals/moe/External Web Services Guide V_2026-MAY.pdf. (3) Map/case-search UI with "Circular Area" (lat/long radius), Structure Class, Sponsor, Height AGL, Date Created filters and CSV export at https://oeaaa.faa.gov/oeaaa/oe3a/main/#/search. Two things you must build yourself and are the entire moat: hand-digitized fence-line polygons for ~130 US refineries (crude/FCC/coker/reformer unit sub-polygons from satellite imagery), and a crane-model → lift-capacity lookup table (Demag CC2400 ~400 t, CC2800 ~600 t, Liebherr LR1600 ~600 t, Manitowoc MLC650 ~700 t, Demag CC6800 ~1,250 t, CC8800 ~1,600 t, Deep South VersaCrane TC28000 ~2,500 t, TC36000 ~3,000+ t).
- **The bet / trade:** Long the regional refined-product differential over the crane's stated work window, sized by crane tonnage class. Primary instrument: CME-cleared regional grade spreads — for PADD 2 signals (Robinson, Toledo, Whiting, Joliet, Lemont) buy "Chicago Unleaded 87 Gasoline (Platts) vs. RBOB Gasoline" or "Chicago ULSD (Platts) vs. NYMEX Heating Oil"; for PADD 3 signals buy the Gulf Coast equivalents. Secondary, higher-beta leg when two or more heavy-lift filings cluster in one PADD in an overlapping window: long the outright front-month crack, RB minus CL 1:1 (gasoline-making units: FCC, reformer, alky) or HO minus CL 1:1 (distillate units: coker, hydrocracker, DHT); CDU filings get the 3-2-1. Entry: on the filing's appearance (same day via the REST API, or within 6 days off the Saturday bulk refresh). Exit: at WORK SCHEDULE ENDING DATE. Median hold 2-5 months (median filing-to-crane-mobilization 47 days, median stated crane window 116 days). Add or roll on a PRIOR ASN revision that pushes the end date out — that is the turnaround overrunning. Cut on a revision that pulls it forward or on free text revealing a capital project rather than a turnaround.
- **Mechanism:** A refinery turnaround is fundamentally a rigging problem. Pulling a coke drum, replacing an FCC regenerator cyclone, lifting a hydrocracker reactor head, or re-traying a fractionator requires a crawler crane whose boom stands 200-500 ft over the unit. That boom is an aviation obstruction, so 14 CFR 77.9 obliges the operator of the equipment to file FAA Form 7460-1 before erecting it. The filing must state where (lat/long to the arcsecond), how tall, and for exactly what dates. Consequently the *rigging plan* — which is settled long before any public statement about maintenance — becomes a federal public record. Chain: crane vendor files 7460-1 → you geolocate it to a specific process unit inside the refinery polygon → crane tonnage class tells you lift scope (400 t = exchanger bundles and routine work; 2,500-3,600 t = reactor/regenerator/drum replacement, i.e. a multi-month unit outage) → unit identity tells you which product yield is impaired (FCC/reformer/alky → gasoline; coker/hydrocracker/DHT → distillate; CDU → both) → WORK SCHEDULE dates give you the outage window → regional supply of that product tightens for that window → the regional grade differential and the crack widen. The verified example: three filings entered 2026-01-23 at Marathon Robinson, IL — "Manitowoc 2250 at Crude / West Side of Crude Unit within MPC Refinery" and "Manitowoc MLC650 at 90C-2 / East of Unit 90" — with work windows 2026-03-02 to 2026-06-01. That is a crude-unit turnaround at a 250 kb/d Chicago-feeding refinery, unit-named, on the federal record 39 days early.
- **Who's on the other side:** Crack-spread specs, CTAs, physical blenders and refiner hedgers who price maintenance off four channels, all of which are slower or coarser. (1) IIR Energy PECWeb / Wood Mackenzie (ex-Genscape) turnaround calendars — built from operator surveys and voluntary disclosure, so they give a quarter and a region, rarely a specific unit, and almost never a credible duration or a slip. (2) Reuters/Bloomberg "sources familiar with plant operations" stories, which usually land at or after shutdown. (3) EIA Weekly Petroleum Status Report refinery utilization, published Wednesdays with a ~5-day reference lag — a coincident indicator, not a leading one. (4) Refiner earnings-call and 10-Q language, which is deliberately vague ("planned maintenance at a Gulf Coast facility"). The structural reason the FAA channel escapes all of them: the 7460-1 is filed by the *crane vendor* — Deep South, Buckner Heavylift, Maxim, Barnhart, Bigge, ALL Erection — not by the refiner. It sits entirely outside the refiner's disclosure control, outside investor relations, and outside the state air-permit channels (TCEQ emissions events, LDEQ incident reports) that energy analytics shops already scrape. The refiner's comms team does not know a federal filing naming their coker exists.
- **Information lead:** Measured directly on 39 refinery-adjacent crane filings in ASW 2024-2026 within 3 km of a refinery: median 47 days from ENTERED DATE to WORK SCHEDULE BEGINNING DATE; p25 19 days, p75 87 days, p90 169 days; 51% filed 45+ days ahead, 26% filed 90+ days ahead. Concrete verified cases: ExxonMobil Baton Rouge VersaCrane TC36000 filed 2025-11-26 for a 2026-05-14 to 2026-08-14 window — 169 days. ExxonMobil Beaumont "Versa Crane CC28000" filed 2026-04-30 for 2026-11-09 to 2027-03-09 — 193 days. PBF Toledo "TC36000 - PBF" (450 ft) filed 2026-05-04 for 2026-07-01 to 2027-01-31 — 58 days on a seven-month heavy-lift window. Marathon Robinson crude unit filed 2026-01-23 for 2026-03-02 — 39 days. Against the market's actual first read this is roughly 3-8 weeks ahead of press confirmation of the turnaround, 6-10 weeks ahead of the EIA utilization print that reflects it, and 1-6 months ahead of the unit-level scope and duration ever becoming public at all. The separate and sharper lead is the extension channel: a PRIOR ASN revision or an FAA extension request pushing the crane's end date out fires days-to-weeks before an overrun is publicly known, and overruns are precisely what surprise the crack.
- **Why still under-exploited:** It is an aviation-safety database, and its entire user base is wireless carriers, wind developers, and airspace consultants. No energy data vendor packages it: turnaround intelligence is sold from operator surveys (IIR), satellite thermal and SAR (Kayrros, Orbital Insight, Ursa), and state emissions-event filings (TCEQ, LDEQ) — three channels that are all orthogonal to federal crane notices. The friction is severe and is the moat. Refinery-adjacent crane filings are a genuine needle: AGL region 2026 alone contains 3,398 crane cases in 20,689 rows, of which ~9 sit near a refinery; ASW 2026 has 1,242 crane cases of which 13 are within 3 km of a refinery. There are no facility identifiers, no unit codes, and no operator field — the refinery attribution exists only as raw lat/long plus free-text like "XOM Coker" or "TC36000 - PBF", so you must hand-build fence-line polygons and a crane-model tonnage table before a single row is usable. The bulk files are misleadingly named .gzip while being CSV. And the 2025-26 government-shutdown banners and service interruptions on the OE/AAA portal make the feed look unreliable enough that no commercial vendor has bothered to productize it.
- **Rough +EV:** Roughly 40-80 refinery-adjacent crane filings per year nationally across all eight FAA regions; after filtering to heavy-lift class (>=1,000 t) or explicitly unit-named filings inside a fence-line polygon, about 12-20 actionable signals per year. A single large-unit outage at a PADD-relevant refinery typically moves the regional grade differential 3-12 c/gal ($1.25-$5.00/bbl) and the outright crack $1-3/bbl over the outage window. Realistically you capture a fraction: assume $0.50-$1.50/bbl of edge on the regional differential leg per correct signal, hit rate 55-62% (the shortfall is sign risk from capital-project false positives, not noise), and 15 signals a year — order $3-8/bbl of gross annual edge on the leg, or a few hundred bps on a modestly levered overlay book. This is a high-conviction, low-frequency overlay on an existing refined-products book, not a standalone strategy: at 15 signals a year you cannot diversify away a bad attribution. The highest-conviction sleeve is the extension channel (PRIOR ASN chains plus FAA extension requests), where a schedule push-out is near-unambiguous and the market is genuinely blind — but it fired only 4 times in 39 filings in my sample.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The data source, the fields, the API, the bulk endpoint, and the lead-time distribution are all directly verified against live FAA data, and the signal-to-unit mapping demonstrably works (three Marathon Robinson filings naming the crude unit and Unit 90, a Valero Houston filing naming the FCC unit, an ExxonMobil Beaumont filing naming the coker). The biggest uncertainty is attribution sign risk: distinguishing a turnaround crane (bullish crack over a 1-4 month window) from a capital-project or expansion crane (neutral now, bearish crack in 1-3 years). My own scan produced exactly this failure mode — four 400 ft cranes at "BP Whiting" whose LOCATION DESCRIPTION reads "Existing site is a golf course" with a 2027-2029 window, and a Baton Rouge filing that is actually the "Shell Blue Cat Project" lifting modules at a neighboring chemical site. Get that wrong and you are long the crack into a capacity addition. Secondary uncertainties: small N (~15 tradeable signals/yr), and silent turnarounds — maintenance that never needs 200+ ft of boom generates no filing at all, so absence of a filing is not evidence of absence of an outage.
