## 46. The 30-Day Tariff Docket

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Freight derivatives (FFAs, trucking). RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** China's mandatory container liner published-rate filing docket (公布运价备案) held by the Shanghai Shipping Exchange. Under the PRC Regulations on International Maritime Transportation and MOT implementing measures (carriers since Aug 2009, NVOCCs since Dec 2010, cross-strait since Mar 2013), every liner operator serving Chinese ports must file its published tariff with SSE, and — critically — "备案的公布运价自受理之日起满30日生效": a filed published rate only becomes legally effective 30 days after acceptance. The docket is therefore not a static tariff book, it is a forward calendar of who intends to raise Asia–Europe rates, by how much, effective when. ~950 million filed records from 150+ carriers as of end-2025. The signal is the daily DIFF of that docket, not the level.
- **How to collect it:** Public query portal, no login: http://www1.sse.net.cn/newfiling/openprice.jsp (legacy JSP form). Query fields: 航线 (route — select 欧洲/Europe), 船公司 (carrier: Maersk, MSC, CMA CGM, COSCO, Hapag, ONE, Evergreen, HMM, Yang Ming, OOCL, ZIM…), 起始港 (Shanghai, Ningbo, Qingdao), 目的港 (Rotterdam, Hamburg, Antwerp, Felixstowe, Le Havre), 箱型/箱子尺寸 (40HQ = FEU), 货类, 生效日期 date range, 公布运价范围. Results table returns rate, commission, box discount, BAF/CAF, THC and 生效日期. Crawl daily: ~12 carriers × 3 POL × 5 POD × 40HQ ≈ a few hundred polite requests/day; snapshot to Parquet and diff vs. prior day. Build three series keyed on FUTURE effective date: (1) breadth = number of top-12 carriers with a newly filed increase clustered on one effective date; (2) magnitude = FEU-weighted median filed level and step size vs. the prior effective window; (3) revisions = filings amended down or superseded before they take effect. Cross-reference the same site's 运力备案 (capacity/service filing) section for concurrent service suspensions or blank-sailing-driven deployment changes. Entirely sanctioned collection — this is a government-mandated public disclosure portal.
- **The bet / trade:** INE (Shanghai International Energy Exchange) EC futures — Container Shipping Index (Europe Service), cash-settled to SSE's weekly SCFIS Europe. LONG the front/second EC month when the docket shows a wide, large, clustered filing (≥8 of top-12 Asia–Europe carriers file an increase on the same effective date, weighted median filed step ≥15% vs. prior window). SHORT/fade when the effective date is approaching with breadth <4 carriers, or when filings are amended downward or quietly superseded. Entry at T-30 to T-25 versus the filed effective date; exit into the first two SCFIS prints after the rate takes effect — a 3–6 week hold. Institutional access to EC is via the QFI/RQFII route (INE expanded QFI-eligible contracts effective 4 March 2025). Same view expressible with less access friction, and against an even slower crowd, in EEX SCFIS-settled container futures, SGX container freight futures, or CME FBX01 as a correlated proxy leg.
- **Mechanism:** SCFIS is a SETTLED-rate index: it prints what shippers actually paid on voyages that have already departed, which is why it can be cash-settled and why EC futures track it mechanically. Actual Asia–Europe FAK spot pricing is negotiated as a discount off the filed published tariff, and Chinese law makes charging outside a filed rate (published or filed agreement rate) unlawful — so the filed tariff is the legal ceiling that must be raised BEFORE a general rate increase can be pushed through. A carrier that wants a GRI effective 1 September must have the number sitting in a public database by roughly 1 August. Breadth of filings is therefore a direct 30-days-ahead census of how many carriers have committed to attempting the increase, and the filed step size is the ceiling they are reaching for. Wide + large filings → the GRI sticks → SCFIS steps up 1–2 weeks after the effective date → EC settles higher. Thin filings, or filings amended down before effect, → the GRI collapses within days of launch → SCFIS rolls over. The docket converts an intention that is normally invisible until the carrier emails its customers into a dated, quantified, public forward calendar.
- **Who's on the other side:** EC front-month flow on INE is dominated by Chinese retail and domestic trend/CTA money — the contract is notorious for herding and limit-move days — plus forwarder and BCO hedgers who are price-takers on timing and trade off headline news. They learn a GRI exists from carrier customer advisories and from Loadstar / Alphaliner / Linerlytica coverage roughly two weeks before it bites, then from the SCFI and SCFIS prints after. The Western analytics stack (Xeneta, Drewry, Freightos, Linerlytica, Sea-Intelligence) is built on quoted and booked rates scraped from carriers and forwarders, plus announced GRIs — i.e., on the carriers' own downstream broadcast, not on the upstream regulatory docket that legally has to precede it by 30 days. Nobody is diffing this database daily because the professional consensus is that published tariffs are fiction ("nobody pays tariff"), which is true about the LEVEL and irrelevant to the SIGNAL, which lives in the filing calendar and breadth.
- **Information lead:** Statutory 30 days ahead of the rate's legal effective date. Roughly 14–16 days ahead of the carriers' customer-facing GRI announcements, which cluster at about two weeks' notice. Approximately 5–7 weeks ahead of the SCFIS print that actually settles the futures. Practically, 10–20 trading days of clean lead over the mainstream freight-press narrative that currently moves EC, and the lead is structural rather than a scraping race — it is created by the acceptance-to-effect rule, so it cannot be competed away by faster crawling.
- **Why still under-exploited:** No vendor sells it. There is no Western product built on SSE filing data — Kpler, Vortexa and Signal are AIS/vessel-flow; Xeneta and Freightos are rate-quote panels; Linerlytica and Alphaliner are capacity and schedule analysts. The portal is Chinese-language, on a legacy JSP form with no API, no bulk export, and query-by-carrier pagination, so building the panel is tedious manual work rather than a data purchase. The prevailing analyst prior — that published tariffs are meaningless because everyone trades off contract and FAK rates — actively discourages anyone from looking, and that prior is defensible about levels while being wrong about the timing calendar. And the EC contract has only existed since August 2023, so there is no incumbent sell-side research franchise whose job it is to have already found this; the filing system predates the futures contract by fourteen years and was built for consumer-protection oversight, not for anyone trading a derivative.
- **Rough +EV:** Asia–Europe GRI attempts cluster around the 1st and 15th, giving roughly 15–24 candidate events per year, of which a breadth-and-magnitude filter is decisive on maybe 8–12. EC front-month realized vol is very large (annualized roughly 60–100%, with single-day limit moves), and GRI-sticks versus GRI-fails typically separates the subsequent SCFIS path by 10–30% over the following month. Base rate of a GRI fully sticking is near a coin flip; if docket breadth moves that to ~65–70% conditional accuracy, then at 1x notional per event against a ~12% average absolute move you capture roughly 2–4% per event gross, i.e. ~20–35% annualized gross on deployed notional, standalone Sharpe plausibly 0.8–1.2. Haircut hard for EC bid-ask, INE margin and price-limit gap risk, QFI access cost, and for the ~2 weeks of the 30-day lead that evaporates the moment carriers announce publicly. The trade is cheap to run — one crawler and one analyst — so even a materially degraded version clears its cost.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty: whether filed published tariffs are genuinely informative about intent to push spot, or have decayed into pro-forma compliance filings parked at absurd ceilings that carriers never bother to revise — in which case breadth would be noise. The related risk is the confidential channel: 协议运价 (filed agreement/service-contract rates) take effect ~24 hours after filing and are NOT publicly queryable, so if the real Asia–Europe money has migrated entirely to that channel the published docket may lead nothing. Both are cheaply falsifiable before a dollar is risked: the portal exposes 生效日期 ranges historically, so pull 2023–2026 filings for the top 12 Asia–Europe carriers, build the breadth and magnitude series, and regress them against subsequent SCFIS Europe step changes and against EC settlement prints. If the backtest shows filings are static or lag announcements, the idea dies in a week for the cost of one crawler.

---
## 47. The Tariff Sheet Ladder

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Interest rate & inflation event contracts. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** State public-utility-commission tariff dockets and default-service procurement auction results — the exact residential rate schedules, riders, and their legal effective dates. These are not a proxy for CPI electricity; per the BLS household-energy factsheet, they ARE the literal input. BLS prices CPI electricity and utility (piped) gas by taking each sampled utility's rate schedule (fixed service charge + consumption charge) and computing the bill for a fixed consumption quantity supplied by DOE, collected monthly. The tradable core is the calendar of approved step-changes: (1) restructured-state default-service resets — NJ BGS-RSCP, MD/DE/DC Standard Offer Service, PA quarterly Price-to-Compare, OH SSO, IL IPA, MA Basic Service, CT Standard Service, ME Standard Offer; (2) fully-regulated-state fuel adjustment clause / purchased gas adjustment / ERRA compliance filings and rate-case final orders; (3) PJM Base Residual Auction capacity clears ($269.92 then $329.17/MW-day) flowing into those default-service prices on known dates.
- **How to collect it:** Verified live and free. (a) bgs-auction.com — NJ BGS auction results, run by PSE&G/JCP&L/ACE/Rockland, announced by the BPU "within 2 days of the end of the BGS-RSCP Auction" each February. (b) State e-docket portals, all public and scrapable: Ohio dis.puc.state.oh.us, NY documents.dps.ny.gov, CA apps.cpuc.ca.gov, Texas PUC Interchange, Illinois ICC e-Docket, MA DPU FileRoom, CT PURA, MD PSC, FL/GA PSC — pull final orders and compliance tariff sheets by docket type (FAC, PGA, ERRA, default service). (c) NREL/OpenEI Utility Rate Database, api.openei.org/utility_rates?version=8 with a free registered key, returns machine-readable residential tariffs with effective dates and seasonal/tiered structures as the backbone and cross-check. (d) FERC eLibrary and PJM's public auction reports for the wholesale pass-through. (e) EIA-861 annual customer counts plus BLS's published 75-area definitions and relative-importance tables to weight utilities into CPI index areas. Output: a synthetic tariff-only CPI electricity/utility-gas index, run forward 1-6 months on already-approved effective dates. Everything is a government or utility institutional filing, published for public inspection — no individuals, no scraping of private data, no non-public sources.
- **The bet / trade:** Buy the specific headline CPI bucket the tariff model displaces consensus into, on monthly CPI event contracts (Kalshi headline CPI m/m and y/y ladders; ForecastEx CPI), entered once the last relevant docket order is approved — typically 2-8 weeks before the BLS release — held to settlement on release day. Because energy is headline-only, the cleanest expression is the headline-versus-core pair: long the displaced headline bucket, flat or offsetting core. Institutional expression for size: the single-month CPI fixing via zero-coupon inflation swaps on NSA CPI-U for the specific reference month, or short-dated SR3/SOFR options straddles bought into the print only when modeled deviation from consensus exceeds ~5bp on headline m/m. Highest-conviction fires are the June print (released mid-July, capturing the June 1 Mid-Atlantic default-service resets known since February) and the January and July prints (capturing MA/CT/ME Jan 1 and Jul 1 basic-service resets approved 45-60 days earlier).
- **Mechanism:** US retail utility prices do not drift — they step, on legally fixed effective dates set by a public regulatory order. BLS converts those tariffs into CPI mechanically: rate schedule in, fixed DOE consumption quantity applied, price out, every month. So the CPI electricity index for month M is close to a deterministic function of documents that already exist and are public before month M begins. Electricity is 2.489% of CPI and piped gas 0.773% (Dec 2025 RI), so a 5% miss on the electricity index alone is roughly 12bp on headline m/m — larger than the typical consensus error on the whole print. A second, subtler edge: because BLS holds consumption FIXED at a DOE average, weather-driven usage swings do not enter CPI electricity at all. Only the tariff does. Nowcasters who regress CPI electricity on heating/cooling degree days are modeling bills, not the index, and are actually picking up seasonal rate-block structure — which the tariff schedules already state explicitly. The tariff-only model should strictly dominate the weather-and-gas-futures model.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side inflation strategists and CPI event-contract market makers who nowcast household energy from Henry Hub and PJM/ERCOT forward power, degree-day anomalies, and last month's EIA print — plus retail Kalshi flow anchored to the Bloomberg consensus median. They are structurally slow for three reasons. First, the only public hard data on US retail electricity prices is EIA's Electric Power Monthly, which runs ~2 months behind: the May 2026 average retail price by state and sector was released July 23, 2026, after both the May and June CPI prints. There is no fast public read. Second, regulatory lag decouples retail tariffs from wholesale power for 6-18 months, so gas-futures-driven nowcasts systematically miss the step changes in both timing and size — and in 2025-26 that relationship is outright broken by data-center load growth and the PJM capacity price shock passing through on fixed dates. Third, the source data lives in roughly fifty mutually incompatible state docket systems as scanned PDFs of tariff sheets. That is exactly the ugly, unglamorous, un-automatable work a macro research desk will not staff for a 3.3%-weight headline-only component.
- **Information lead:** Real and long, varying by state. NJ BGS: auction results announced early-to-mid February for rates effective June 1 — roughly four months before the price change and five months before the June CPI print lands in mid-July. MA Basic Service and CT Standard Service: filed and approved ~45-60 days before Jan 1 and Jul 1 resets, so ~2.5-3.5 months before the relevant print. Fully-regulated-state rate case final orders and FAC/PGA compliance filings: 30-90 days before effective date. PA quarterly Price-to-Compare: 30-45 days. Blended across the sample, you hold a hard, exact-percentage read on roughly 60-70% of the electricity index four to ten weeks before the market's information set contains anything but a wholesale-power extrapolation, and you hold it two full months before EIA publishes any confirming retail number.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Tariff data is commercialized — Genability, Arcadia, URDB — but exclusively for solar developers, energy-software vendors and utility bill analytics. Nobody sells a PUC-docket-to-CPI-item-index mapping, and no inflation nowcast product (Cleveland Fed, sell-side energy models, the standard CPI nowcast trackers) uses regulatory dockets as an input; they all use gas futures plus EIA. The mapping layer — utility service territory to BLS pricing area, rate schedule to BLS item stratum, EIA-861 customer counts to relative-importance weights — has to be built by hand and has no off-the-shelf buyer, which is precisely why it does not exist. The CPI-electricity nowcast community is also tiny because energy is headline-only and most desks trade core, so the component is chronically under-modeled relative to shelter or used cars. And the current regime makes it unusually live: the PJM capacity shock and data-center load growth are driving an unprecedented wave of double-digit default-service resets, so the steps are large, the historical wholesale relationship is broken, and everyone else's model is mis-specified at exactly the moment the signal is biggest.
- **Rough +EV:** Electricity plus piped gas is 3.26% of CPI. Monthly NSA CPI electricity m/m has a surprise standard deviation of roughly 1.0-1.5%; a tariff-only model should cut the conditional error 50-70% in the ~5-7 months a year when a large regional reset or a cluster of FAC/rate-case effective dates lands (June, July, January, plus PA quarterly months). That translates to a 3-6bp expected shift in headline CPI m/m versus consensus on a typical fire, and 10-15bp when a big June 1 Mid-Atlantic reset hits. On Kalshi headline m/m ladders with 0.1pp buckets priced 15-45c, a durable 4-8bp directional edge in the underlying is worth roughly 4-10 cents per contract on the adjacent bucket — call it 8-15% ROI per fire, firing 5-7 times a year, so roughly 40-90% annual return on capital-at-risk before fees. Capacity is the binding constraint, not edge: Kalshi CPI books are thin, realistically $25-100k per event; the ZC inflation swap expression scales far better but pays 2-4bp bid/offer on single-month fixings, which eats a third to half the edge on the smaller fires. Only trade the swaps when modeled deviation exceeds 8bp.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty by far: BLS does not publish its utility sample. You know the tariffs exactly, but not which utilities and which specific rate schedules are in the CPI sample, nor their within-area weights — you have to infer them from EIA-861 residential customer counts mapped onto BLS's 75 pricing areas, and that sampling/weighting error could plausibly consume 30-50% of the modeled edge. Backtest against published area-level CPI electricity indexes before sizing; if the historical R-squared on the tariff-only reconstruction is under ~0.7 at the area level, the edge is not there. Second uncertainty, and it is a real one: headline CPI m/m event contracts settle on the seasonally adjusted number, while your model produces NSA. Electricity seasonal factors are large and BLS re-estimates them every February, so a perfectly correct NSA call can still land in the wrong SA bucket. Mitigation is to prefer the y/y ladders, which settle NSA, and to hold m/m positions only when the modeled move clears the seasonal-factor revision band.

---

## 48. The Ad Council Minute

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Advertising & media buying. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection (public APIs/filings/own sensors) — no anti-bot evasion, harvested credentials, side channels

- **Data source:** The literal composition of national ad pods on E.W. Scripps' broadcast diginets (ION, Bounce, Grit, Laff, ION Mystery, ION Plus), captured off-air from your own rooftop antenna. The measured quantities are the ones no commercial ad dataset sells because advertisers don't pay to see them: (1) Ad Council PSA seconds per hour — inventory that generated literally zero revenue; (2) house-promo seconds per hour; (3) the share of commercial seconds sitting in units of :60/:120 rather than :15/:30 — the length signature of direct-response/per-inquiry buys; (4) scheduled "Paid Programming" half-hours per week from the ATSC PSIP guide embedded in the same stream; (5) count of distinct national brand advertisers per hour and the tier composition of the DR roster (legal lead-gen, insurance aggregators, supplements = bottom of the rate card). Critically, Scripps migrated Bounce, Grit, Laff, ION Mystery and ION Plus onto ION station subchannels, so one RF multiplex from a single ION O&O carries the entire Scripps Networks portfolio's national feed — the exact revenue-generating inventory, not a local approximation.
- **How to collect it:** Hardware: SiliconDust HDHomeRun Flex 4K or Quatro (~$180) plus a UHF antenna aimed at the local ION owned-and-operated station (WPXN-TV New York, KPXN-TV Los Angeles, WPXP/etc.). All Scripps nets ride the same ION multiplex, so 4 tuners cover the whole portfolio. Pull the raw transport stream continuously off the device's local HTTP endpoint (http://<device>/auto/v5.1 style URLs) into ffmpeg — ~2-5 Mbps per subchannel, trivially cheap storage at 24/7. Segment pods with ffmpeg blackdetect + silencedetect + loudness-boundary detection to cut individual spots. Classify each spot by: duration bucket; audio fingerprint (Chromaprint/acoustid or open-source dejavu) against a persistent spot library you accumulate — Ad Council PSAs are a small fixed library and fingerprint perfectly; and tesseract OCR of lower-thirds for 1-800 numbers, "call now," "results not typical" to flag DR. Parse PSIP EPG tables straight out of the same stream for the Paid Programming census. Zero third-party terms of service involved: this is free over-the-air spectrum received by your own sensor, analyzed privately and never redistributed. Cross-check the quarterly ground truth against Scripps' 10-Q Networks segment disclosure on EDGAR (CIK 0000832428), which explicitly breaks out direct-response pricing impact.
- **The bet / trade:** Short SSP (E.W. Scripps Class A) equity, or buy 1-2 month put spreads bracketing the earnings date, entered roughly 2-3 weeks before the print once ~80% of the quarter's pod composition has been observed; reverse to long on a positive inflection. Horizon: enter mid-to-late quarter, settle on the earnings print — roughly 5-8 weeks per cycle, 4 cycles a year. Preferred expression is the pair: short SSP against a beta-matched long in a pure station-group basket (NXST/GTN), which strips out retrans and political-ad beta that drives Scripps' Local Media segment but not Networks, isolating the segment your antenna actually measures. Secondary expressions: SSP's term loan and unsecured notes, where the equity is a thin sliver under ~$2.5B of debt and Networks segment profit is the swing factor for any distressed-exchange catalyst.
- **Mechanism:** Networks revenue = impressions delivered × price of the marginal sold impression. Broadcast avails are perishable — an unsold minute is gone forever — so a network facing weak brand demand walks a strict fill ladder: sell :30s to national brands, then dump into direct-response :60/:120 units at per-inquiry or near-zero effective CPMs, then paid programming half-hours, then house promos, then Ad Council PSAs when nothing at all clears. The pod's composition is therefore a direct, continuous readout of where on that ladder the network is sitting, sampled at the moment of transaction rather than 6-14 weeks later at the 10-Q. Scripps' own Q2 2026 filing confirms the transmission channel explicitly: Networks revenue fell 16% to $172M with segment profit collapsing from $55.9M to $25.5M, and management attributed 7.9 points of the decline specifically to lower direct-response pricing (plus 9.2 points to ratings/Nielsen methodology). Rising PSA seconds, rising ≥:60 share, rising paid-programming hours, and a thinning distinct-brand-advertiser count within a quarter mechanically precede a Networks revenue miss and the guidance cut that follows it.
- **Who's on the other side:** Three slow counterparties. (1) The handful of small-cap broadcast analysts who model Networks off Nielsen ratings plus management's qualitative color. Nielsen measures audience, not monetization mix — and Q2 2026 proved the gap when a Nielsen methodology change alone moved revenue 9.2% and nobody had it modeled. (2) Institutional ad-spend datasets. Guideline/SMI, the standard tool, sources actual agency billing and captures 70-95% of agency spend — but direct-response and per-inquiry inventory is bought direct by DR specialist shops and by advertisers themselves, outside agency billing entirely. The single line item destroying Scripps' P&L is structurally invisible to the dataset everyone uses. (3) iSpot/Vivvix track brand ad occurrences for advertiser clients and deliberately exclude or undercount PSAs, house promos and paid programming — precisely the inventory that signals distress — with thin diginet coverage and no packaging as an equity nowcast. Meanwhile the marginal holder of a ~$3 levered microcap is index/passive and retail, which reprices only on the print.
- **Information lead:** Full-quarter real time versus a ~38-day post-quarter-end report (Q2 ended June 30, reported August 7). Practically: you know the direction and rough magnitude of the Networks revenue trajectory 6-8 weeks before the print and up to ~4 months before the quarter's earliest revenue is disclosed, and you front-run the forward guide given on the same call. Versus sell-side channel checks the lead is effectively infinite, because for DR inventory there is no channel to check — no syndicated measurement of per-inquiry pricing exists in any form. The only faster observer is Scripps' own ad sales desk.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Four things keep this empty. First, the target is a sub-$500M-cap levered broadcaster — nobody with a data budget builds infrastructure for it, and the funds that could are capacity-constrained out of the name. Second, the signal requires physical RF collection: an antenna, a tuner, and a video pipeline, which is deeply unfashionable next to web scraping and card data, and which most quant shops have no competence to stand up. Third — the core asymmetry — every commercial ad-occurrence dataset in existence is built and sold to advertisers, who want to see competitors' brand campaigns. PSAs, house promos and paid programming are systematically stripped out as noise. The waste inventory is the entire signal, and the industry has spent thirty years engineering it out of their products. Fourth, direct response is the last major ad market with no syndicated spend or price measurement at all, and it happens to be the marginal clearing market for exactly this class of asset. This is not a repackaging of a mainstream feed; the underlying observation does not exist as a product anywhere.
- **Rough +EV:** SSP earnings-day realized moves have been running ±15-30%, and with Local Media in an off-cycle political year the Networks line is the dominant swing factor. Assume the composition signal calls the direction of the Networks revenue surprise 62-68% of the time — it captures volume and mix cleanly but not price. Sized to the print with modest-delta put/call spreads, that implies roughly +3% to +8% expected return on notional per event, across 4 earnings prints plus 1-2 guidance or credit catalysts a year — call it 15-40% annualized on deployed capital, before slippage. Capacity is the hard binding constraint: SSP trades a few million dollars a day, borrow is tight, options are wide and thin. This is a $1-5M idea, not a $50M one. The same antenna rig throws off a free second signal — a general linear-scatter and DR-pricing nowcast usable on Nexstar/Gray/Sinclair diginet exposure and on the CTV-versus-linear rotation narrative — at zero marginal cost.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty by far: pod composition observes quantity and mix but not price, and price is where the damage actually happens — Scripps can hold DR share flat while DR CPMs collapse, which is close to what it disclosed for Q2 2026. Partial mitigation is that the DR roster's tier composition is a usable price proxy (drift toward legal lead-gen, insurance aggregators and supplements indicates rate-card capitulation) and PSA seconds are unambiguous zero-revenue, but a quarter where mix is stable and only price moves will give a false negative. Second-order risks: portfolio actions distort reported revenue in ways the antenna cannot see (the Court TV sale alone cut $8.5M from Q2), so the signal must be applied strictly like-for-like on surviving networks; and single-market OTA capture assumes the national feed is what you receive, which holds for diginets but should be validated against a second-market tuner before sizing.

## 49. The Turn-Up Tell

**Arena:** Financial-market primary (listed publisher equity + single-name options), with a secondary gambling leg on Polymarket/Kalshi game-release-date and "delayed again?" binaries.
**Domain:** Video games & interactive entertainment. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** Game publishers' own network-provisioning footprint, read out of four operator-facing institutional registries that nobody in finance touches: (1) PeeringDB records for each publisher ASN — every IXP port and its speed in Gbps (`netixlan`), every colo/interconnection facility (`netfac`), each with an `updated` timestamp; (2) Euro-IX IXPDB, which auto-ingests IX-F Member Export JSON straight out of ~700 IXPs' management systems (IXP Manager etc.) and is therefore fresher and less self-report-biased than PeeringDB; (3) RIPE NCC's RIPEstat / RIS + RouteViews, giving the full history of which prefixes an ASN originates, from where, and when they first appeared; (4) RIR daily `delegated-extended` files (ARIN/RIPE/APNIC), which date-stamp every new IPv4/IPv6 allocation to the org. Verified real ASNs with live records: Take-Two Interactive AS394977, AS46555, AS202021; Riot Games AS6507; Roblox AS22697 (and AS11281 corporate); Sony Interactive Entertainment AS33353; Ubisoft AS22634 and Ubisoft France AS8361; Nexon AS17766; Electronic Arts AS14891. The composite series is "committed Gbps + PoP count + originated prefixes per publisher per week."
- **How to collect it:** Nightly cron, all free and sanctioned public APIs, no scraping of gated pages. PeeringDB documented REST API: https://www.peeringdb.com/api/net?asn=394977 , /api/netixlan?net_id=… , /api/netfac?net_id=… (free API key via account). Euro-IX IXPDB: https://ixpdb.euro-ix.net/ — pull the registered IX-F Member Export list from its API and fetch each IXP's member JSON (schema at github.com/euro-ix/json-schemas); parse for your target ASNs' port count and speed at each exchange. RIPEstat Data API, no key: https://stat.ripe.net/data/announced-prefixes/data.json?resource=AS394977 and /data/routing-history/data.json?resource=AS…&starttime=… . RIR files: ftp.arin.net/pub/stats/arin/delegated-arin-extended-latest (plus RIPE/APNIC equivalents), diffed daily for new allocations to the org handle. Backtest history is already archived: CAIDA's PeeringDB Archive, https://publicdata.caida.org/datasets/peeringdb/ — daily full-database snapshots, SQL/SQLite from 2010 and full JSON API dumps from 11 Mar 2018 onward. The only manual work is the ASN→issuer map: resolve each publisher's subsidiaries and legacy ASNs via bgp.tools / bgp.he.net org search and RIR whois org handles, and maintain it by hand (Take-Two alone spans three ASNs; Ubisoft is split France/global; Sony has SIE separate from Sony Online Entertainment AS13720).
- **The bet / trade:** Live focal trade: Take-Two (TTWO) into the GTA 6 launch on 19 Nov 2026 — today is T-14 weeks, exactly inside the provisioning window. Measure the T-16w→T-4w delta in committed Gbps, new PoPs, and newly originated prefixes across AS394977/AS46555/AS202021 plus new ARIN allocations to the Take-Two org handle, versus that same publisher's own trailing 3-year baseline. Hot trace (top-decile buildout, new metros lit): long TTWO Dec-2026 / Jan-2027 calls, held through launch and the FQ3 FY27 print in early Feb 2027. Flat trace at T-10w (mid-September 2026): buy TTWO Dec-2026 puts and take the "no" side on Polymarket/Kalshi "GTA 6 releases in 2026." Systematic version: a recurring launch-window overlay across the ~15 publishers that run their own networks — Nexon 3659.T, Krafton 259960.KS, NCSoft 036570.KS, Pearl Abyss 263750.KQ, Ubisoft UBI.PA, Roblox RBLX, Capcom 9697.T, Square Enix 9684.T, Sea/Garena SE — entered 20-60 trading days before a dated major launch and exited 10 days after.
- **Mechanism:** A publisher cannot serve more concurrent players than it has provisioned for, and provisioning is not instantaneous: cross-connect orders run 2-6 weeks, IXP port turn-ups 1-4 weeks, RIR allocations 1-4 weeks, transit contracts 30-90 days. So the capacity a publisher commits in the quarter before a launch is sized directly off its confidential internal peak-CCU and day-one-download forecast — the single most valuable private number in the building. That forecast leaks into public peering and routing registries as a side effect of coordinating with other networks. Crucially it is a costly signal, not cheap talk: ports, cross-connects and transit are contracted spend that is painful to unwind, so unlike trailers, wishlist counts and CEO reassurance it cannot be inflated for free. Two readings fall out. Scale: buildout far above the publisher's own historical norm means management privately expects a monster; a hype-heavy launch with no matching buildout means they privately expect modest concurrency. Timing: production environments get stood up on a fairly rigid runway, so a trace that has not begun by roughly T-10w says the shipping date is not real yet — which front-runs a late delay announcement.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side gaming analysts and event-driven funds who model launch scale off trailer view counts, Steam wishlist ranks, digital-storefront pre-order chart positions, retail channel checks and Circana/GSD data — all of which measure consumer *attention*, none of which measure what the publisher itself has budgeted for. On the delay binaries, the counterparty is a retail crowd trading off leaker accounts and "insider" tweets, plus the reflexive read-through from company reassurance (Take-Two has publicly reaffirmed 19 Nov 2026 twice). The people who can actually see this data — peering coordinators and network engineers at IXPs, transit providers and the publishers themselves — have no trading interest, and the registries exist for peering coordination and academic AS-topology research. Nobody on either side has ever built the series.
- **Information lead:** Capacity commitments appear in the registries roughly 4-14 weeks before the game ships, because that is the physical turn-up runway. The market gets its definitive read on launch scale either on launch day (peak concurrents, social chatter) or at the following earnings call — for Take-Two that is early February 2027, nearly three months after launch. A delay is learned only when the publisher announces it. So the lead is about 1-3 months on the scale question and, for a late slip, several weeks on the announcement. Within-quarter for live-service names, the same trace gives an engagement read months before the quarterly disclosure.
- **Why still under-exploited:** No vendor sells ASN-level capacity or routing time series mapped to equity tickers. Kpler and Vortexa do vessels, YipitData and M Science do cards and app data, Ortex does borrow — the peering layer is a total blind spot because it sits with network operators, not with any financial data supply chain. The raw feeds are free and documented but hostile to use: PeeringDB self-reports go stale and must be cross-checked against IXPDB's automated IX-F exports; publishers hide behind multiple ASNs and subsidiaries so the issuer mapping is hand-built; and you must filter out cloud/CDN offload. CAIDA's daily archive back to 2010 exists purely because internet-measurement academics wanted AS-topology history — the backtest data has been sitting in the open for sixteen years with no financial consumer. Note also that Take-Two's own networks are small today (AS394977 peers with only ~22 networks), which means a GTA 6-scale buildout would be enormous in relative terms and trivially detectable once you are watching.
- **Rough +EV:** Fires roughly 15-25 tradeable launch windows a year across the own-ASN publisher universe, plus four quarterly nowcast decisions per name. On the equity leg I would expect a 55-60% directional hit rate on launch-window drift against typical 3-6% moves — call it 1-2% expected return per trade before costs, made meaningfully convex if expressed in options rather than stock. The concentrated EV is in the binaries: if a credible provisioning read shifts P(GTA 6 slips out of 2026) by 10-15 points against a market currently pricing something like 10-20%, that is a 10-15 point edge on a single settled bet, which dwarfs a year of the equity overlay. Realistically this is a low-single-digit-Sharpe standalone signal and a genuinely good sizing/confirmation overlay on launch trades you were going to consider anyway.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty by far is cloud/CDN offload blindness: a publisher that serves entitlement, matchmaking and downloads from AWS/Azure/Akamai/Cloudflare/Fastly can mount a colossal launch with zero growth in its own ASN footprint, and Rockstar leans heavily that way — so a flat trace is ambiguous between "management expects modest scale" and "it is all outsourced," which is exactly the wrong ambiguity for the short/delay leg. Mitigate by scoring each publisher's own-network share first and only trading names where the historical trace actually tracked past launches. Secondary risks: PeeringDB staleness (use IXPDB as primary, PeeringDB as cross-check), and a thin backtest — maybe 30-60 clean megalaunch events since the JSON archive starts in March 2018, which is not enough to pin the effect size tightly.

---
## 50. The Objection Window

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Cloud & enterprise software telemetry. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** Public GDPR Article 28(2) sub-processor lists and their dated change-notice changelogs, harvested vendor-centrically instead of customer-centrically. Every enterprise SaaS company is contractually obliged to publish a current, named inventory of every third party that touches customer personal data — and, per its own DPA, to post additions/removals with 15–30 days ADVANCE notice and a customer objection window before the change takes effect. Read in reverse, this is a public, contemporaneous, legally-maintained customer roster for every infrastructure vendor named on those pages: CDN (Fastly, Inc. / Cloudflare, Inc. / Akamai), comms (Twilio Inc., SendGrid, Inc.), observability (Datadog, New Relic, Sumo Logic), auth (Okta/Auth0), data (Snowflake, MongoDB, Confluent, Elastic), and now inference (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google Vertex, AWS Bedrock, and the middleware layer — Baseten, Fireworks AI, Together AI, Modal). Confirmed live example: Sierra's trust center posted a forward-dated notice adding Baseten, Fireworks AI, Together AI and Modal as inference sub-processors; Pantheon names "Fastly" as a platform sub-processor. A vendor appearing = contract entering production. A vendor disappearing = contract already terminated, disclosed before it takes effect.
- **How to collect it:** Four ungated, uniform corpora plus a bespoke tail. (1) Trust-center platforms: Vanta (documented public endpoint api.vanta.com/v1/trust-centers/{slug}/subprocessors, fields name/purpose/location/url — see developer.vanta.com/api-reference/trust-centers/list-trust-center-subprocessors), SafeBase/Drata (<slug>.safebase.io + custom trust.<co>.com CNAMEs), Sprinto (<slug>.trust.site/subprocessors, trust.<co>.ai), Conveyor. Enumerate slugs by querying crt.sh for %.safebase.io / %.trust.site and by CNAME sweeps of trust.<domain> across a domain list — CT logs give you the full tenant census for free. (2) Each trust center's /updates changelog (e.g. trust.sierra.ai/updates) which carries the dated, forward-effective notices. (3) ~500 hand-mapped bespoke pages for the mega-caps: trust.salesforce.com/en/subprocessors, slack.com/trust/compliance/subprocessors, legal.hubspot.com/subprocessors, atlassian.com/legal/sub-processors, explore.zoom.us/en/subprocessors, docs.fastly.com/products/sub-processors, twilio.com/en-us/legal/sub-processors, aws.amazon.com/compliance/sub-processors, Microsoft's Online Services Subprocessor Disclosure PDF, plus PDF lists (e.g. allvuesystems.com subprocessor-list.pdf). (4) Opt into the vendors' own official change-notification feeds — explicitly offered and therefore explicitly sanctioned (docs.fastly.com/changes emails sub-processor changes; most trust centers have "subscribe to updates"). Backfill without waiting: Wayback CDX API (web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=<page>&output=json&fl=timestamp&collapse=digest) returns every unique-content capture of each page going back years — that is the historical panel for backtesting. Then normalize legal-entity strings ("Fastly, Inc.", "Twilio Inc.", "SendGrid, Inc.") to tickers, and weight each mention by the PUBLISHING company's revenue/employee count. Hard rule for sanctioned collection: crawl only ungated pages and opt-in public feeds; skip any trust center behind an NDA click-through or email gate. Daily poll, ~5k pages, trivially within one small box.
- **The bet / trade:** Single-name equity, 1–2 quarter horizon, expressed in options. Primary: when a vendor's revenue-weighted net mention count across a FIXED cohort of trust pages falls more than ~1.5 sigma over a rolling quarter, buy 2–4 month put spreads on that vendor struck near the post-guidance level, held through the next one-to-two earnings prints; partially fund with calls on the named displacing vendor. Target universe is customer-concentrated small/mid-cap infrastructure where one logo moves the print: FSLY (top-10 customers = 33–37% of revenue per its 10-K and 2026 calls), TWLO, ESTC, AMPL, BRZE, CFLT, OKTA, DDOG, MDB, NET. Settlement: the earnings date at which NRR/DBNRR and the forward guide land. Secondary book: the inference sub-processor census as a revenue-weighted share series (Azure OpenAI vs Vertex vs Bedrock vs the Baseten/Fireworks/Together/Modal middleware layer), traded as MSFT/GOOGL/AMZN cloud-segment relative value into the quarter, and as directional exposure to neocloud names when the middleware layer gains share against the hyperscalers.
- **Mechanism:** The sub-processor list is a compliance artifact that happens to be a contract ledger. To name a third party there, a company must have executed a data-processing agreement with it and be about to put it into production; to remove one, the contract must already be terminated or the data flow already cut. The DPA's own notice clause forces the disclosure to be published BEFORE the change takes effect, because the customer must be given a window to object. So the tape is a bookings-and-churn feed with a legally mandated forward stamp. Revenue lags it mechanically: a terminated consumption contract bleeds off over the remaining term and only surfaces in the vendor's aggregate, smoothed net-revenue-retention figure one to three quarters later, at which point the market repricesed a "usage headwind from a large customer" it could have read on a public webpage months earlier. In the reverse direction, an addition is a signed deal already entering production, months before the first full quarter of revenue. Concentration is what converts the signal into a move: at 33–37% of revenue in ten logos, Fastly's guide is a function of a handful of relationships that are each individually observable on the customer's own trust page.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side analysts and long-onlys modelling NRR from company-reported aggregates and quarterly channel checks, plus the alt-data complex, none of whose panels see this. Card panels do not see enterprise contracts. Web/app traffic panels do not see B2B infrastructure at all. Tech-stack products (BuiltWith, HG Insights, Datanyze, Wappalyzer) detect front-end tags and DNS on public marketing sites — they can see which CDN fronts a homepage, but not which vendor holds the contract for private, API, and edge workloads, and they see nothing whatsoever of the SendGrid / Segment / Okta / Snowflake / inference-provider class of relationship, which never touches the browser. Crucially, the people who DO collect this exact data are pointed the wrong way: GRC and third-party-risk tooling (PageCrawl.io, Whistic, UpGuard, Prevalent) monitors sub-processor pages for compliance alerting, sold to security teams, priced per-page for a company's own 50–500 vendors. It is a customer-centric alerting workflow with no cross-vendor aggregation, no revenue weighting, no entity normalization, and no financial product. Nobody runs it in reverse. Vendors themselves are slow to be forced into disclosure: most infrastructure swaps are never press-released by either party.
- **Information lead:** Three separate leads stacked. (1) Versus the effective date: 15–30 days, structurally, because the objection window must precede the change — you read the future-dated notice before the contract actually flips. (2) Versus the vendor's own disclosure: one to three quarters, since churn only appears inside an aggregate NRR figure after the terminated term runs off, and is never attributed to a named logo. (3) Versus any press release: frequently infinite — the majority of CDN, comms, observability, auth and inference vendor swaps are simply never announced by anyone, and the trust page is the only public record that the relationship ever existed or ended. Against competing alt-data, the lead is not a matter of days but of visibility: no card, web, app, or job-posting panel resolves a B2B data-processing contract at all.
- **Why still under-exploited:** It is a legal-compliance byproduct with zero marketing intent, published on pages investors have no reason to visit, in thousands of bespoke formats plus PDFs. Turning it into a panel requires three unglamorous steps nobody has stitched together: tenant enumeration across four trust-center SaaS platforms via certificate-transparency sweeps, legal-entity-string normalization to tickers, and revenue-weighting of the PUBLISHING side so a two-person startup's mention does not count the same as Shopify's. The obvious tooling exists but points the other way — it is sold as compliance page-diff alerting to security teams. And the corpus only recently became machine-readable at scale: the Vanta/SafeBase/Drata/Sprinto trust-center boom is roughly three years old, the documented subprocessor APIs newer than that, and the AI-inference entries (the second engine here) barely two years old. There is also a psychological moat: the data looks like boilerplate legalese, which is exactly why it has been left alone.
- **Rough +EV:** Panel of ~4,000–6,000 ungated trust pages. Realistic firing rate after cohort-fixing and noise filtering: roughly 15–40 vendor-level signals per year across ~25 tradeable names, of which perhaps 8–15 are clean enough to size. Honest hit rate on the direction of the next NRR/guide surprise: 58–65% — real but not overwhelming, because you only observe the subset of a vendor's customers that both publish a list and name entities rather than categories. Typical move on a guide or NRR miss in this cohort is 12–25%, and these names gap rather than drift. Sized as put spreads where a miss costs ~1x and a hit pays 2.5–3x, that is roughly +20–40% expected return per trade, call it 4–9 percentage points of annualized alpha on deployed capital for a two-person team. Build cost is the real drag: two to four months of engineering for the crawler, entity normalization and Wayback backfill, plus continuous breakage as pages get rewritten. Capacity is small — this works at $5–50M, not at $2B.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The single biggest uncertainty is denominator instability and coverage bias: the population of companies publishing sub-processor lists is itself growing fast as trust-center adoption spreads, so raw mention counts drift upward for reasons unrelated to any vendor winning. You must diff only on a fixed cohort of pages present and parseable at both endpoints — and even then a removal can reflect a page rewrite, a narrowing of disclosure scope, a shift from named entities to generic categories ("cloud hosting provider"), or a corporate-entity rename, rather than a real contract termination. False-positive removals are the failure mode that kills this, and each one has to be adjudicated by hand against the Wayback diff before it is traded. Secondary risks: trust centers migrating behind NDA gates (which must be dropped from the panel entirely, shrinking coverage over time), and the possibility that the largest customers of the target vendors are disproportionately the ones that disclose only by category.

---
## 51. The Patch Half-Life

**Arena:** financial-market (US/global equities: single-name security & infrastructure software vendors; expressed in shares or 1-2 month options)
**Domain:** Cybersecurity incidents & disclosure. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** The Shadowserver Foundation's free public Dashboard (dashboard.shadowserver.org) — specifically two panels almost nobody in finance touches: (1) "Attack statistics: Vulnerabilities" honeypot telemetry, which reports daily unique source IPs and connection attempts per exploited CVE from a global sensor net, with a built-in "Anomalies" mode that flags a CVE the day mass exploitation begins; and (2) the "combined/vulnerable/compromised-devices/web-cves" scan datasets, which report the daily count of internet-exposed AND already-compromised instances of a given product, broken out by country and by ASN. Two years of history, no account required. The derived variable that nobody publishes: the **decay half-life of the exposed population** for a specific vendor product after a vuln goes hot — i.e., how fast the installed base actually patches — plus the compromised-to-exposed ratio. Cross-referenced against CISA KEV (verified live: known_exploited_vulnerabilities.json, catalog 2026.08.11, 1,665 entries) and CISA's Vulnrichment git repo (github.com/cisagov/vulnrichment), where the SSVC "exploitation: active" flag is committed with a git timestamp before the KEV listing.
- **How to collect it:** All free and sanctioned. (a) Poll dashboard.shadowserver.org/statistics/honeypot/vulnerability/monitoring/ (Category=Anomalies, Statistic=Unique IPs) and .../time-series/ daily; the series are embedded as JSON in the page HTML (~1MB), one request per panel per day. Shadowserver also issues a free Dashboard API key on email request for programmatic access. (b) Poll .../statistics/combined/time-series/ and .../combined/tree/ for the vulnerable / compromised / web-cves datasets, grouped by ASN, to get exposed-base counts and decay curves. (c) Pull CISA KEV JSON (cisa.gov/sites/default/files/feeds/known_exploited_vulnerabilities.json) and git-log github.com/cisagov/vulnrichment + CVEProject/cvelistV5 for commit-timestamped exploitation flags. (d) Build the two crosswalks that constitute the actual moat: honeypot tag → CVE → NVD CPE → vendor → ticker (hand-built, ~300 rows covers the listed universe), and ASN → corporate entity via ARIN/RIPE/APNIC bulk WHOIS + PeeringDB + CAIDA AS-to-Org. Total infra: one cron job, a SQLite file, and about a week of manual mapping work.
- **The bet / trade:** Single-name equity, event-driven, 4-8 week horizon. Trigger: a CVE in a listed vendor's internet-facing flagship product goes "sustained" — honeypot unique-IP anomaly persists >5 days AND exposed-base decay half-life >21 days AND absolute exposed instances >~5,000. On that signal: SHORT the vendor's stock or buy 1-2 month 5-10% OTM puts, held through the next earnings print, paired long against the direct replacement vendor (e.g., long ZS/NET/PANW against a compromised VPN/edge appliance vendor) to strip sector beta. Universe skewed to single-product small/mid caps where the multiple is fragile: NABL (N-able, two N-central KEV adds Aug 3-4 2026), PRGS, FFIV, CVLT, TENB, RPD, QLYS, ATEN, OKTA, plus large caps FTNT, CSCO, PANW, ORCL, AVGO(VMware), CHKP, AKAM, TEAM, 4704.T. Mirror leg: when the honeypot spike decays inside ~72h and the exposed base is small, BUY the vendor's knee-jerk 2-5% dip, hold 2-3 weeks. Second-order leg for the ASN panel: a sustained compromised-device count inside an *operating* company's own ASN is a 2-8 week lead on that company's own incident disclosure.
- **Mechanism:** A CVE advisory is a non-event; a mass-exploitation campaign is a P&L event. The difference is the size of the vulnerable installed base and how slowly it patches, because that determines how many downstream victims are created, which in turn determines whether the story is a two-day trade-press item or a MOVEit — a multi-quarter drip of victim disclosures, state AG breach filings, class actions, regulatory overhang, customer churn and a multiple de-rating. Causal chain: honeypot sensors see exploitation attempts on day 0 → the exposed-instance count and compromise ratio tell you the addressable victim pool → a long patch half-life means the victim pool keeps converting into actual breaches for months → each downstream victim generates a disclosure that re-prints the vendor's name in a breach headline → institutional buyers pause deals and renewals slip → billings/NRR deteriorate one to two quarters later. The market prices the advisory. The data prices the campaign.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side software and security analysts and generalist event-driven desks, who react to the vendor's advisory, the trade press, and the CISA KEV addition — and who have no way to size the installed base, so they default to "vulnerabilities happen to everyone, buy the dip." Passive/index holders who never react. Options market makers who mark the initial 2-3% gap as the entire event and sell the front-month vol into it. They are slow for a structural reason: the differentiated data lives on a security nonprofit's dashboard with zero financial framing — no tickers, no vendor rollup, no bulk CSV, JS-rendered pages, and a taxonomy of honeypot tags and CPE strings that means nothing to a finance data team. The crosswalk from honeypot tag to ticker does not exist anywhere and has to be built by hand. Meanwhile the people who read Shadowserver fluently — CERTs, ISP abuse desks, SOC analysts — are culturally and often contractually non-trading, and Shadowserver's per-network detail is delivered privately to the network owner, so there is no leakage path into markets.
- **Information lead:** Three distinct leads. (1) On the fact of mass exploitation: 2-10 days ahead of the CISA KEV dateAdded and of mainstream trade-press coverage, because KEV requires evidence plus an assigned remediation action plus internal process; the Vulnrichment "exploitation: active" git commit is an intermediate checkpoint that itself precedes KEV by days. (2) On scale and duration — the actual alpha: 2-8 weeks ahead of everyone, because the exposed installed base, the compromise ratio and the patch decay half-life are numbers the vendor will never publish and the sell-side never estimates. Nobody else knows on day 3 whether 800 or 80,000 instances are hanging out there and whether they are patching in a week or a quarter. (3) On the financial consequence: one to two quarters ahead of the vendor's own disclosure of churn, billings or NRR impact, and ahead of the downstream victim wave that shows up later in state AG breach registries and 8-Ks.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Shadowserver is a grant-funded nonprofit whose product is free remediation reporting to network owners and CERTs; it has no commercial incentive to package data for investors, no historical bulk download, and no vendor or ticker dimension. The public dashboard is deliberately aggregated to country/ASN level and rendered client-side, which reads as "not a dataset" to a quant team scanning for vendors. The adjacent commercial products all point away from this use: GreyNoise, VulnCheck, Censys and Recorded Future sell exploitation and exposure intel to SOCs and IR teams on security-budget contracts, priced and documented for defenders, and none of them publish a per-vendor-product exposed-base decay curve with ASN attribution for free. The alt-data industry's cyber coverage is almost entirely the obvious layer — leak-site victim scraping and 8-K Item 1.05 parsing — which is now bot-tweeted within minutes and carries no lead. The genuine barrier is not access, it is the two hand-built crosswalks (honeypot tag→CPE→vendor→ticker, and ASN→corporate entity), which are unglamorous, must be maintained manually, and sit exactly at the seam between two professions that do not talk to each other.
- **Rough +EV:** Roughly 10-15 events per year where a listed vendor's internet-facing flagship product enters real mass exploitation (consistent with ~180-245 KEV additions/year, of which Cisco, Ivanti, Fortinet, Oracle, Palo Alto, Citrix, VMware, SonicWall, SolarWinds, Progress and N-able account for a large share of the edge-device subset). Expect ~4-6 to classify as "sustained" and ~6-8 as transient fades. On the sustained shorts, target 3-6% absolute capture over 4-8 weeks on the pair, with the tail cases (F5 Oct 2025 ~-12% in a day; SolarWinds ~-17%; Okta ~-11%; Progress a multi-quarter de-rating) supplying most of the return — a right-skewed distribution where roughly two trades a year carry the book. Fade leg adds 1-3% on smaller size. Realistic gross ~8-18% annually on a dedicated sleeve at a ~60-65% hit rate, with high variance and a real chance of a flat year. Capacity is the binding constraint: small/mid-cap tilt plus event scarcity caps this around $20-75M before slippage. Marginal cost is near zero — one cron job and a SQLite file — so the Sharpe on effort is far better than the Sharpe on capital.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The data source, its freshness, its granularity and its free/sanctioned status are verified directly, and the information lead over KEV and over the press is real and measurable — you can backtest it exactly, since both Shadowserver history and KEV dateAdded are free and timestamped. The biggest uncertainty is not the signal, it is the transmission: whether vendor equities reliably discount mass exploitation of their own product at all. The market has largely decided that vulnerabilities are a cost of doing business for entrenched incumbents, so Fortinet and Cisco frequently absorb a KEV listing with no move whatsoever, and the edge may concentrate in a thin subset — single-product small/mid caps, and the specific case where the vendor's own corporate network (not just its customers' deployments) is compromised. Secondary risks: GreyNoise Trends is close enough to this that some of the day-0 exploitation signal may already be in the tape, and Shadowserver's honeypot coverage is uneven by protocol, so absence of a spike is weaker evidence than presence of one. Before sizing, backtest the last 24 months of KEV additions against Shadowserver history and vendor stock reactions — that test is free and will settle the transmission question in a week.

---
## 52. The Splisting Ladder

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Payments & card networks. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** The Visa Global Registry of Service Providers — the full 9,018-entity registry of every third party Visa has vetted to touch its rails, pulled as structured JSON rather than as a compliance lookup. Per entity you get: legal entity name, Visa Business ID (BID), country/state/city, `registeredSince` date, `regionList` (U.S./EU/AP/LAC/CEMEA/CAN/GLOBAL), the QSA that assessed them, and — buried one level down inside `validationTypeList[].serviceProviderTypeList` — the service-provider TYPE taxonomy. Verified live counts as of today: PAYMENT FACILITATOR 1,696 (235 U.S.-region), MERCHANT SERVICER-VISA 1,399, ISO-MERCHANT 2,482, THIRD PARTY SERVICER 1,556, VISANET PROCESSOR 635, ISO-HIGH INTEGRITY RISK 466, HIGH INTEGRITY RISK PAYMENT FACILITATOR 214, MARKETPLACE 25, CRYPTO RAMP PROVIDER 16. New registrations are accelerating: 616 (2022) → 803 (2024) → 1,113 (2025) → 810 in 2026 through August. Visa even prefixes sanctioned entities in-band, e.g. "(EU Sanctioned) BelarusianRussian Belgazprombank JS". Cross-confirm against Mastercard's monthly SDP Compliant Registered Service Provider List PDF, where a provider is dropped 90 days after its sponsoring Mastercard customer stops submitting its PCI AoC.
- **How to collect it:** Undocumented, unauthenticated JSON API behind Visa's public search SPA. I reverse-engineered it from the minified Angular bundle at https://www.visa.com/splisting/main.js (base path `/splisting/api`). Working calls, no key, no login: (1) `GET https://www.visa.com/splisting/api/fetchCriteria` returns the full controlled vocabulary — every service-provider type, program, region, and assessor. (2) `POST https://www.visa.com/splisting/api/searchGrsp`, Content-Type application/json, body `{"searchRequest":{"companyName":null,"programs":null,"serviceProviderType":null,"regions":null,"assessor":null},"page":N,"size":200}` — note `page`/`size` must sit at the TOP level of the body; nesting them inside `searchRequest` or passing them as query params is silently ignored and pins you to 30 rows, which is the trap that stops casual scrapers. ~46 pages walks the entire registry in under two minutes. (3) `GET /splisting/api/serviceProviderDetail/{spId}` for per-entity detail. Critical operational point: `lastModifiedDate` is a single uniform bulk stamp — 08/12/2026 across all 9,018 rows — so there is no "what's new" feed. You must store your own weekly snapshot and diff spId × serviceProviderTypeList × regionList yourself. Collection is sanctioned: Visa publishes this registry expressly so merchants and acquirers can verify providers; there is no paywall, credential, or auth bypass involved. Keep pulls to weekly and low-concurrency. Mastercard mirror at mastercard.com/content/dam/public/mastercardcom/globalrisk/pdf/SP-Post-List-MM-DD-YY.pdf (needs a real browser UA; naive curl gets 403).
- **The bet / trade:** Weekly snapshot diff, three event types, all in listed equities (long leg via common or 6-12 month calls).

(A) TYPE FLIP — an entity holding only MERCHANT SERVICER-VISA / ISO-MERCHANT adds PAYMENT FACILITATOR. Buy the parent, hold 2-4 quarters into the take-rate step-up. Verified as NOT yet flipped today, so all still live: ServiceTitan (TTAN) — "ServiceTitan, Inc", registered 1/21/2019, U.S., ISO-MERCHANT + MERCHANT SERVICER only; Tyler Technologies (TYL) — MERCHANT SERVICER-VISA only; EverCommerce (EVCM) — ISO-MERCHANT + MERCHANT SERVICER only. Contrast with peers that already flipped: AppFolio (APPF), Toast (TOST), Lightspeed (LSPD), Wix (WIX), Shopify Payments USA, Intuit.

(B) NEW NATIONAL ENTITY — a new legal entity in a country the parent didn't previously cover. Long the parent, 2-3 quarters. Live and, as far as I can find, unannounced right now: Circle (CRCL) registered "Circle Vietnam Technologies Company Limited" on 6/30/2026 (AP, ISO-CARDHOLDER) — six weeks old, no Vietnam press release found. Same pattern already visible at EBANX (Chile 5/29/2026, Uruguay 1/26/2026, Dominican Republic 12/16/2025), dLocal/DLO (two Argentina PayFac entities Jan + Apr 2025), Nuvei (Brazil PayFac 3/10/2025), PagSeguro/PAGS (Peru 4/30/2025 as HIRPF + PayFac — a Brazil-only name in the market's mind quietly standing up Peru).

(C) DELIST / TYPE LOSS — an spId disappears or drops a validation type between snapshots. Means the sponsoring acquirer stopped registering them or PCI revalidation failed; either way they cannot process. Short or underweight the small-cap ISO/PSP, 1-2 quarters.

Horizon: long leg 2-4 quarters; short leg 1-2 quarters.
- **Mechanism:** Registration is a legal prerequisite, not a marketing act. A company physically cannot facilitate Visa transactions for sub-merchants in a region until an acquirer sponsors it and Visa lists it. So the registry row is the earliest externally visible artifact of a payments go-live and necessarily precedes first revenue — the causal arrow runs registration → go-live → revenue, never the reverse.

The type flip is the largest revenue-per-customer step change in vertical software. A SaaS company that refers payments to a sponsored partner earns a rev-share on roughly 15-40bps of customer GPV. Becoming the registered Payment Facilitator makes it merchant-of-record: it now bills the merchant the full ~250-300bps, pays interchange + scheme fees as COGS, and keeps the spread — call it 60-90bps net, several times the referral economics on identical volume. On $10B of customer GPV that is a nine-figure revenue line appearing where there was a modest rev-share, and it re-rates the multiple because payments revenue is where these names' bull cases live.

For cross-border PSPs the mechanism is corridor count. dLocal, EBANX, Nuvei, PagSeguro, Circle grow by adding countries; each new local entity is a new revenue corridor with its own licensing and settlement stack. "New markets added" is precisely what the sell-side models, and the registry dates it before the press release.

Delisting runs the other way and is unusually clean: to stay listed a provider needs both an active sponsoring Visa client and a current PCI DSS AoC. Mastercard's list states the rule explicitly — removal 90 days after the sponsoring customer goes past due. Losing sponsorship means losing the ability to process at all, which is existential for a monoline ISO/PSP and shows up in the diff before it shows up in a going-concern paragraph.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side software and payments analysts model "payments attach" from management guidance and investor-day slides — they learn on the company's schedule, by construction. Vendor-risk and compliance teams are the registry's actual audience, and they use it as a point-in-time yes/no lookup on one named vendor; nobody in that seat has any reason to keep a longitudinal panel. The proof is that third parties republish frozen PDF snapshots of the registry ("as of March 31, 2025", "January 26, 2024") and treat them as current — the industry regards this as a static list, not a time series.

Quant and alt-data shops index card-transaction panels, app downloads, and job postings; regulatory registries are unfashionable because they don't look like a panel and don't come with history. And there is genuinely nothing to buy here: no bulk export, no changelog, no vendor product. Corporate IR won't help either — a registration isn't material until launch, so there's no disclosure obligation and no incentive to confirm.

The people who could beat you to it — acquirers and Visa itself — see the flow but can't trade on it.
- **Information lead:** Against the press release: roughly one to two quarters. My cleanest live evidence is Circle's Vietnam entity, registered 6/30/2026, with no Vietnam announcement I can find as of 14 Aug 2026 — six weeks of lead already banked and counting. The same shape recurs across EBANX's Chile/Uruguay/Dominican ladder, dLocal's Argentina entities, and PagSeguro's Peru registration.

Against the P&L the lead is much longer: registration → build and go-live → first full revenue quarter → reported quarter is typically three to five quarters. That is the window in which you are holding a position on a fact the tape has not seen.

The lead is structural rather than a race, which is the important part. Because `lastModifiedDate` is a uniform bulk stamp with no per-record change timestamp and no diff feed, the event only exists relative to a snapshot you took earlier. Someone who discovers this endpoint tomorrow sees a static list of 9,018 rows and learns nothing about what just changed. The lead therefore cannot be competed away by a faster crawler — only by someone who started before you did.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Five compounding reasons, each verified rather than assumed.

(1) No documented API and no bulk export. The public UI is a 30-row-per-page Angular SPA; I had to pull and grep the minified bundle to find `/splisting/api`. A web search on this exact question returns "the registry appears to be available for manual searches rather than bulk data scraping" and "no mention of an official API."

(2) A deliberate-looking pagination trap. `page`/`size` work only at the top level of the POST body — as query params or nested inside `searchRequest` they're silently ignored and you're capped at 30 rows. Anyone who tries the obvious things concludes the registry can't be bulk-pulled. I hit this three times before it worked.

(3) No change feed, so no catch-up trade. All 9,018 rows carry the identical `lastModifiedDate` of 08/12/2026. History cannot be bought or reconstructed; it only exists if you built it. This is the moat and also the cost of entry.

(4) The valuable field is nested and invisible to shallow scrapers. Service-provider type lives inside `validationTypeList[].serviceProviderTypeList`, not on the top-level record — so even someone scraping the rendered search results captures company names and dates while missing the entire taxonomy that carries the signal.

(5) It takes domain knowledge to see why it matters. You have to already know that MERCHANT SERVICER → PAYMENT FACILITATOR is a several-fold change in revenue per merchant, that HIGH INTEGRITY RISK is Visa's gambling/adult/pharma designation under VIRP, and that CRYPTO RAMP PROVIDER is a brand-new 16-entity category. The audience that reads this registry (compliance) has no reason to know that; the audience that would know it (payments investors) has no reason to read a PCI compliance list.

Not sold by Yipit, M Science, Kpler, Vortexa, Ortex, or anyone else I could find, and no evidence of any maintained longitudinal panel.
- **Rough +EV:** Base rates measured directly from the pull, not guessed: 9,018 entities; ~1,113 new registrations in 2025 and 810 in 2026 YTD (≈1,300 annualized); 1,696 Payment Facilitators of which 235 are U.S.-region.

Filtering to entities attributable to a US/EU-listed parent, expect roughly 15-40 actionable events a year — call it 4-10 type flips at listed vertical-SaaS names and 15-30 new-country entities at listed PSPs, plus a handful of delistings.

Sizing per event. A PayFac flip at a mid-cap vertical-SaaS name is worth something like 15-40% of re-rating over 4-8 quarters once the payments line is visible; capturing the first third of that ahead of disclosure, at a realistic 55-60% hit rate, is on the order of 4-8% expected return on the position. Country-add events are smaller and noisier — 1-4% moves at DLO/PAGS/CRCL-type names — and should be run as a basket tilt, not a single-name bet, because a meaningful share of registrations are defensive or dormant and never monetize. Delistings are rare but the payoff is convex when they land on a monoline.

Portfolio contribution: plausibly 2-5% annualized alpha on a 10-20 name payments and vertical-SaaS book, with most of it concentrated in the two to four flip events per year. The asymmetry is that running cost is essentially zero — a weekly cron job, ~46 API calls, a few megabytes of JSON — so the strategy is +EV at very low hit rates. The real cost is calendar: you must run it for two to three quarters before you own any tradeable diffs.
- **Confidence:** Medium.

The source itself is verified beyond doubt — I pulled all 9,018 records live today through an open unauthenticated endpoint, confirmed the taxonomy via `fetchCriteria`, and identified specific registrations (Circle Vietnam 6/30/2026, EBANX Chile 5/29/2026, PagSeguro Peru 4/30/2025) that appear to have no corresponding public announcement.

The single biggest uncertainty is that the type-flip signal is not backtestable. `registeredSince` records when an entity first appeared in the registry, not when a given service-provider type was added to it — so a company registered in 2015 that becomes a PayFac next month still shows 2015. The flip therefore exists only in your own snapshot diffs, no historical snapshots exist beyond scattered stale third-party PDFs, and you cannot validate hit rate or effect size before committing capital. Everything in my EV estimate above is reasoned from payments economics, not measured. That is the honest weak point.

Secondary risks, in order: entity names frequently don't map cleanly to listed parents (local JV and SPV names like "DEPANSUM (PTY) LIMITED (DLOCAL)" require hand-built mapping and will produce false negatives); absence of a PAYMENT FACILITATOR type does not prove a company doesn't monetize payments today via a sponsored partner, so the flip often signals margin expansion from bringing PayFac in-house rather than brand-new revenue — real, but a smaller effect than a true cold start; registrations can be defensive and never monetize; and Visa could auth-gate or retire the undocumented endpoint at any time, which would push collection back to slow UI-level pulls and materially raise the cost of maintaining the panel.

---
## 53. The Planned Addition

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Banking branch & deposit dynamics. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** Cash-sweep "program bank" rosters and priority-ordered bank lists — living legal disclosure documents published by brokerages and fintechs, not by the banks themselves. The tracked object is the DIFF: additions, removals, and changes in ordinal rank. Verified live: (1) wealthfront.com/programbanks — an HTML roster stamped "Program Banks as of 07/28/2026" listing ~50 named banks, and, decisively, flagging entries as "(planned addition)" — I pulled it and found "Provident Bank (planned addition)" alongside small-caps ConnectOne, Third Coast, Peapack-Gladstone, Metropolitan Commercial, Busey, Customers, Axos, Green Dot, Cathay, Columbia, BankUnited, Centennial, Cross River. (2) LPL Financial's ICA Priority Bank Lists (PBL1/PBL2, separate retail and business PDFs, plus a Deposit Cash Account list) — banks appear in columns by state/region "in the order in which your funds will be allocated," so ordinal position encodes marginal-dollar share, not just membership. (3) Betterment's Cash Sweep program bank list (consolidated into a single program effective 05/01/2026). (4) Robinhood's deposit sweep bank list (Goldman Sachs Bank USA, HSBC, Wells Fargo, Citibank, Bank of Baroda, U.S. Bank). (5) Mercury's Choice Sweep Bank List / Column N.A. and Evolve documents on its legal page. Same pattern at Raymond James (RJBDP), Ameriprise, Pershing IDP, Stifel, Janney, Apex, Altruist, Public, SoFi.
- **How to collect it:** Daily HTTP poll of ~30 stable URLs with content hashing. Confirmed-good endpoints: https://www.wealthfront.com/programbanks ; https://www.lpl.com/content/dam/lpl-www/documents/disclosures/ica-priority-bank-list-retail.pdf ; .../ica-priority-bank-list-business.pdf ; .../deposit-cash-account.pdf ; https://www.betterment.com/cash-portfolio ; https://robinhood.com/us/en/support/articles/deposit-sweep-program/ ; Mercury legal page -> Choice/Column/Evolve sweep bank lists. Parse: pdftotext -layout for the LPL PDFs (must preserve column geometry, since rank is the signal), DOM extraction for HTML. Normalize every bank name to an FDIC cert number via the free FDIC BankFind API (banks.data.fdic.gov/api/institutions), then cert -> RSSD -> holding company -> ticker, so a roster line becomes a tradeable symbol automatically. Backfill and audit history via the Wayback CDX API (web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=...&collapse=digest) — I confirmed snapshots of wealthfront.com/programbanks exist (e.g. 20250511182937); also POST your own observations to web.archive.org/save/ to timestamp them. Calibrate dollars-per-slot retroactively by joining each program's disclosed total sweep balances against each member bank's subsequent FFIEC call report Schedule RC-E brokered/reciprocal/sweep deposit memoranda. Infrastructure cost: one small VPS and a cron job. Entirely sanctioned — these are public documents the programs are legally obligated to keep current.
- **The bet / trade:** Long the small/mid-cap bank holding company (assets under ~$25B) on the day it appears on — or is promoted up — a large sweep roster; short/underweight the one deleted or demoted. Instrument: common equity, dollar-neutral paired against KRE to strip the rate factor; 2-4 month call spreads only for the handful of names with tradeable options. Enter within days of the diff. Hold through the SECOND quarterly print after the change (roughly 60-150 days), because the allocator fills a new slot over one to two quarters, so the first print is only a partial read. Highest-conviction variant: buy on a Wealthfront "(planned addition)" flag, which is a signed-but-unfunded relationship disclosed before a single dollar moves. Secondary expression: short the sub-debt/preferred of a bank removed from a multi-billion-dollar program — losing committed funding is a funding-cost event before it is an EPS event.
- **Mechanism:** Sweep programs are mechanical allocation engines. Wealthfront, LPL, Betterment, Robinhood and Mercury hold tens of billions of client cash and distribute it to a roster of banks in a defined priority order subject to per-bank caps. Because the roster is a compliance disclosure, it must reflect the current arrangement — so it updates at contract effectiveness, and Wealthfront goes further by printing "planned addition" next to a bank that has signed but is not yet receiving flow. The resulting deposits are contractually committed, operationally sticky, and enormous relative to the recipient: one slot on a program with tens of billions of sweep cash spread across ~50 banks is plausibly hundreds of millions to low billions of dollars. For a $4-10B-asset bank that is a step-function improvement in funding cost, deposit beta, and loan-growth capacity — it surfaces as outsized sequential deposit growth, NIM stabilization, and frequently a raised balance-sheet guide. Removal runs the film backwards: committed funding must be replaced at market via brokered CDs or FHLB advances at a worse rate, compressing NIM. LPL's ordinal structure supplies a second, finer signal that pure membership tracking misses entirely: moving from third to first in a state column redirects the marginal dollar with no add or delete at all.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side regional-bank analysts and long-only bank funds, whose entire deposit toolkit is branch-shaped — Summary of Deposits market share, branch counts, rate boards, deposit beta, quarterly call reports. None of that instrumentation can see a contract between a Palo Alto RIA and a Texas bank. The structural blind spot: the disclosure lives on the COUNTERPARTY's website, not the bank's. The bank issues no press release (commercially sensitive, and immaterial standing alone for 8-K purposes), so there is no filing, no headline, and no transcript mention until someone asks "what drove deposit growth?" on the call — at which point it is already in the price. Coverage is thinnest exactly where the signal is largest: the $3-10B banks for whom a slot is transformative carry one or two analysts. And the format is actively hostile — a PDF silently swapped at a fixed URL, with the sharpest information encoded in column position. Diffing compliance PDFs by ordinal rank and joining them to FDIC cert numbers is nobody's job.
- **Information lead:** Layered and real. (a) A "(planned addition)" flag precedes any actual deposit by weeks to a couple of months — this is a pre-funding disclosure.
