(b) An ordinary add/remove posts at contract effectiveness, with balances ramping over the following one to two quarters. (c) The market's first look is the quarterly earnings release and call, which lands 15 to 100+ days after the roster changed depending on where in the quarter it falls; the call report is worse still at 30-45 days after quarter end. Practical lead over the earnings print: roughly 3 weeks to 4 months, median about 6-10 weeks. The lead over analyst COMPREHENSION is longer, because the first print shows only a partial-quarter, partially-ramped balance and gets waved through as "core deposit gathering" — the re-rate typically comes on the second print, which you are already positioned for.
- **Why still under-exploited:** No vendor sells it. Banking alt-data is dominated by rate boards (Curinos, S&P RateWatch), call-report analytics (S&P, Performance Trust), and card/transaction panels (YipitData, M Science) — all branch-and-consumer-shaped. My search of the sweep-data space returned only vendors of sweep PLUMBING (Galileo, IntraFi, Cambr, StoneCastle), i.e. companies that build the pipes, never anyone selling diffs of who is connected to them. The rosters are scattered across dozens of unrelated companies' legal pages in inconsistent formats (HTML table, PDF, embedded viewer) with no change log, no RSS, no API, no version history, and no notification of any kind; reconstructing the past requires Wayback CDX archaeology. It is a manual, unglamorous, cross-domain join — brokerage compliance documents mapped to bank tickers — which is precisely why it sits unclaimed. It also got materially more valuable only recently: post-2023, sweep and BaaS deposits became a primary growth channel for small banks, and roster churn accelerated sharply through the Synapse collapse, the Evolve fallout, BaaS consent orders, and brokered-deposit rule churn. The dataset is young enough that no one has built the history.
- **Rough +EV:** Across ~30 tracked programs, roughly 2-6 material roster events per month, of which perhaps 15-30 per year involve a US-listed bank small enough for the slot to matter — call it ~20 tradeable events annually. For a $4-10B-asset bank, a top-5-program slot is plausibly 3-15% of total deposits. Expected edge on the long leg: about +3% to +6% versus KRE over ~90 days, at a 55-62% hit rate with a fat right tail when management calls out the funding win on the second print. Removals and demotions should pay more and faster, roughly -5% to -12%, since funding loss is punished asymmetrically in small-cap banks. "Planned addition" flags are the highest-quality subset and should sit at the top of that range. Blended, this is a sleeve throwing off a few hundred bps per event about twenty times a year — real, but capacity-constrained to maybe $5-50M by the liquidity of $3-10B-asset bank stocks, not a billion-dollar strategy. Infrastructure runs under $1k/year; the true cost is illiquidity and wide options spreads, so equity-plus-hedge is the honest expression for all but the largest names.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty by far: SLOT SIZE IS UNOBSERVABLE EX ANTE. The roster tells you a bank is in the program and roughly where it sits in the queue, but never its per-bank cap or its actual dollar allocation — and caps vary by an order of magnitude, so one add is $3B and the next is a $100M token relationship. Partial mitigations: LPL's ordinal rank proxies for share, and you can build a per-program dollars-per-slot prior by retro-fitting disclosed program totals against members' subsequent Schedule RC-E deltas. But if a large fraction of adds turn out to be immaterial token slots, the hit rate drags toward a coin flip and the strategy dies — that is the single assumption to test first on backfilled Wayback history before committing capital. Secondary risk: the signal is trivially replicable once known; the moat is obscurity and plumbing, not exclusivity, so assume a 2-4 year half-life.

---
## 54. The Perfection Window

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Private credit & BDCs. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** USPTO patent and trademark ASSIGNMENT recordations filtered to agent-role party strings — records whose assignee/assignor name contains "as Administrative Agent," "as Collateral Agent," or "as Agent" — joined to the named obligors in every listed BDC's Schedule of Investments. Each record carries conveyance type (SECURITY INTEREST / SECURITY AGREEMENT / RELEASE BY SECURED PARTY / TERMINATION OF SECURITY INTEREST / ASSIGNMENT), the assignor entity, the assignee (i.e., the lending syndicate's agent, named verbatim), an EXECUTION date (the actual closing date of the security or release instrument) and a RECORDED date. This is the paperwork exhaust of private-credit deal events — new tranches, refinancings, priming money, and IP drop-downs — sitting in a free federal registry months before the lender's own quarterly filing discloses it. It is not an IP dataset being used for IP purposes; it is a deal-event registry hiding inside one.
- **How to collect it:** Three free, sanctioned, public-government pulls, joined by entity resolution: (1) LIVE/DAILY — USPTO Assignment Center at assignmentcenter.uspto.gov, which accepts URL-parameterized party queries (verified live example: /search/trademark/exactTypeDetails?exactAssigneeName=THE+CIT+GROUP%2FBUISNESS+CREDIT%2C+INC.%2C+AS+ADMINISTRATIVE+AND+COLLATERAL+AGENT — proving agent-role strings are indexed as searchable assignee names); patent side at assignment.uspto.gov/patent. Maintain a standing watchlist of ~120 private-credit agent-name variants (Antares Capital LP, Golub Capital Markets LLC, Blue Owl/Owl Rock, Ares Capital Corporation, Blackstone Alternative Credit, HPS, Sixth Street Specialty Lending, Twin Brook, NXT, Churchill, Monroe, Barings, PennantPark, Silver Point, Diameter, Fortress, Cerberus, each "as Agent"). (2) BULK/HISTORICAL for backtest — USPTO Trademark Assignment Dataset and Patent Assignment Dataset at uspto.gov/ip-policy/economic-research/research-datasets: six linked files (tm_assignment, tm_assignor, tm_assignee, tm_subparty, tm_docid, tm_convey), CSV and Stata, ~272MB, 1.38M assignments over 2.39M properties, back to 1952; plus the separate daily XML assignment feed on the USPTO Data Portal bulk repository for the live tape. (3) DOCUMENT PULL — TSDR API (free API key) retrieves the recorded PDF itself, which frequently names the facility, the tranche, and the borrower group. JOIN KEY — EDGAR: pull every listed BDC's 10-Q/10-K Schedule of Investments (obligor name, industry, security type, rate, principal, cost, fair value, % of net assets), and use EDGAR full-text search (efts.sec.gov) on an obligor name to find every BDC and interval fund holding it. Zero paywalls, zero authenticated scraping, zero MNPI.
- **The bet / trade:** Cross-sectional market-neutral book in listed BDC common equity, rebalanced monthly, held through the next one to two earnings prints (60-150 days). Score each of the ~40 listed BDCs by signal density weighted by each obligor's disclosed % of net assets. SHORT the top-quartile "impairment density" names (new agent's security interest layered over the incumbent = priming/first-out; IP assigned out to a fresh "* IP Holdings LLC" entity then pledged to a different agent = drop-down LME; a distressed shop appearing as agent-of-record on a sponsor-backed borrower = rescue financing) — FSK, PSEC, MFIC, NMFC, OBDC-type books with wide obligor counts. LONG a beta-and-carry-neutralizing basket (BIZD or the bottom-quartile score) to kill the 10-12% dividend/borrow drag that destroys naked BDC shorts. Second, higher-conviction leg: single-name event shorts into the print where one flagged obligor exceeds ~2.5% of a BDC's net assets. Third leg with no dividend drag: sell the same BDCs' unsecured notes / baby bonds (TRACE-quoted) against the sector. Separately and more mechanically: use RELEASE BY SECURED PARTY recordations naming a BDC's own agent as a real-time repayment nowcast to trade the NII beat/miss into that BDC's quarterly print.
- **Mechanism:** Perfection law forces private deal events into public view fast. Under 35 U.S.C. §261 (patents) and 15 U.S.C. §1060 (trademarks), an unrecorded assignment or security interest is void against a subsequent bona fide purchaser unless recorded within three months of the transaction or before the subsequent purchase. Lenders' counsel therefore records IP security agreements at closing as a matter of routine, and — critically — a borrower's NEW lender will not fund until the OLD agent's release is recorded, because clean IP title is a condition precedent. So both the birth and the death of a private credit loan leave a dated federal record within days-to-weeks of the closing. The causal chain to the BDC: (a) a new agent recording a security interest on top of an existing one means new money came in ahead of the incumbent lender — the BDC's loan just got structurally subordinated, which forces a fair-value markdown at the next quarter-end; (b) IP migrating from the OpCo to a newly formed sibling that is then pledged to a different agent is the mechanical signature of a drop-down liability management exercise — the BDC's collateral package just got hollowed out, and these resolve into 30-60% markdowns and non-accrual; (c) a release with no replacement from the same agent means the loan was repaid at par, which shrinks the BDC's earning assets and pulls forward accelerated OID and prepayment fees into the current quarter's NII. The BDC only reveals any of this in its next Schedule of Investments. The registry knows first because the law makes it know first.
- **Who's on the other side:** Listed BDC equity is the most retail-dominated corner of institutional credit — roughly $60-70B of market cap, heavy direct retail and RIA ownership drawn by 10-12% yields, thin passive ownership because BDCs are excluded from most major indices. The marginal price-setter is a yield buyer who has never opened a Schedule of Investments. Sell-side coverage (KBW, RBC, Wells, JMP, Oppenheimer) models BDCs top-down — NAV trajectory, dividend coverage, base rates, spread environment, aggregate non-accruals at cost — and essentially nobody re-underwrites the 100-400 individual obligors in a single book, let alone across forty books. The private-credit specialist press (9fin, Octus/Reorg, Bloomberg) does break LMEs, but their coverage is concentrated in the ~150 largest private borrowers with tradable paper and active lender groups; the typical BDC obligor is a $15-75M EBITDA sponsor-backed company with no rating, no traded loan, and zero press — which is exactly where the lead is longest. Distressed shops and restructuring counsel do pull USPTO and UCC records, but reactively, on a name they are already working — never as a systematic daily registry sweep matched to public lenders' portfolios. And the BDC manager itself, who obviously knows, is the party controlling disclosure timing and is structurally incentivized to hold the mark flat until a hard event compels a change.
- **Information lead:** Six weeks to five months on a specific named obligor; median roughly 2.5 months. The arithmetic: the recorded instrument carries an execution date equal to the actual closing, and recordation typically follows within days to a few weeks because of the three-month perfection clock and the new lender's closing conditions. The BDC discloses the same fact only in its next Schedule of Investments — 10-Q filed 40-45 days after quarter-end, 10-K 60-75 days. A drop-down executed February 10 hits the registry by late February and hits the market in the Q1 10-Q around May 12. Worst case (event closes March 28) the lead is still about six weeks; best case (event closes right after a quarter-end) it approaches five months. Against the specialist press the lead on large names may be near zero, but on the sub-$75M-EBITDA obligors that dominate BDC books there is no press at all, so the registry date is genuinely the first public print.
- **Why still under-exploited:** The two datasets live in non-overlapping professional worlds. IP-assignment data IS sold — by Clarivate/Derwent, PatSnap, Ocean Tomo, IPqwery — but exclusively to intellectual-property professionals, indexed and priced for chain-of-title, freedom-to-operate, and licensing work; their products do not surface conveyance-type transitions, do not parse agent-role party strings, and have no concept of a borrower. Meanwhile the credit alt-data complex (Octus/Reorg, 9fin, Solve, KBRA DLD, LevFin Insights) is document- and human-chatter-driven — it works by cultivating lender-group sources and reading credit agreements, not by sweeping federal registries. Nobody has built the join. The specific unlock — treating the string "as Collateral Agent" inside a USPTO assignee field as a lender identifier, and treating conveyance-type transitions on a single property as a capital-structure event log — is a genuinely odd idea that requires simultaneously understanding trademark recordation practice and BDC fair-value accounting, a combination that is close to nonexistent. It is also unattractive to anyone large: the entity-resolution work is manual and grinding, and capacity in listed BDC equity is maybe $50-200M, far below the threshold that would attract a Point72 or a Citadel pod. Fragmented, manual, small-capacity, and cross-disciplinary is exactly the profile of something left on the table.
- **Rough +EV:** Two distinct engines. (1) REPAYMENT/NII NOWCAST — high frequency, modest edge. Releases fire in the hundreds per quarter across the BDC universe. BDC stocks move 2-5% on NII surprises, and repayment volume plus prepayment-fee/accelerated-OID income is the single most-mismodeled line in sell-side BDC models. Assume ~60-65% directional hit rate versus consensus on ~160 name-quarters a year, at 60-120bps of gross alpha per correct call; net of borrow, dividend replacement on the short leg, and the ~35-40% of calls that are wrong, this is roughly 3-6% annualized on a market-neutral BDC book. (2) IMPAIRMENT EVENT LEG — low frequency, large payoff. Realistically 20-60 genuine priming/drop-down signals a year across all forty BDCs, of which perhaps 8-15 land on an obligor big enough to matter (>2.5% of net assets). A 3% position taken to a 40% markdown is ~120bps of NAV, but BDC equities de-rate 2-4x the NAV hit because the market extrapolates ("if that name, what else is stale in there?"), so these are 5-15% single-name moves. Blended, I would underwrite 8-14% gross annualized on $50-150M of capital, degrading fast above that. Costs are real: BDC borrow is expensive and thin on the small names, which is why the long-basket hedge rather than a naked short is mandatory.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The data source is verified real, free, daily, and legally forced into existence — that part I am confident about. The single biggest uncertainty is ENTITY RESOLUTION AND COVERAGE, and it is the whole ballgame. BDC Schedules of Investments name the borrower/holdco ("Project Alpha Holdings, LLC"), while USPTO records name the IP-owning subsidiary, which is usually the operating brand under a completely different name. If you cannot bridge that gap at scale, you have nothing — and there is no clean crosswalk, so the bridge has to be built from EDGAR full-text, state entity registries, and sponsor portfolio pages, name by name. That is simultaneously the moat and the reason this could simply fail. Two secondary risks that materially cut coverage: first, whole sectors of the BDC book have no recordable IP at all — healthcare-provider rollups, staffing, and pure business services often own no meaningful marks or patents, so the signal is blind on maybe 30-45% of obligors and skewed toward software, consumer brands, and specialty manufacturing; second, RELEASES are recorded less reliably than GRANTS, because once a borrower has repaid, nobody is strongly motivated to chase the paperwork — which biases the repayment nowcast toward false negatives and injects timing noise into engine (1) specifically. I would want a two-year backtest against realized non-accrual migration before sizing this past a pilot.

---
## 55. The Delineated Area

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** REITs & commercial real estate. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** GSA's forward lease-requirement pipeline, reconstructed from three public feeds that nobody joins: (1) SAM.gov Contract Opportunities notices where GSA PBS advertises expiring space requirements — each carries a prose "delineated area" (the street/ZIP boundary inside which offered space must sit), a min/max ABOA square-foot requirement, firm/full lease term, and target occupancy date; (2) GSA's own Lease Inventory — a per-lease roster of every federally leased building (lease number, street address, city/state, agency, rentable/available SF, lease effective and expiration dates), published as a downloadable file and refreshed on a weekly-to-monthly cadence, with the monthly regional version also carrying rental rates; (3) FPDS-NG/SAM contract award records for GSA lease actions (PSC X1AA "Lease/Rental of Office Buildings", NAICS 531120), which name the winning lessor entity and address. The alpha is not any one feed — it is the spatial join: does the delineated area in the new RLP geometrically contain the incumbent building, and is the SF requirement smaller than what the incumbent leases today?
- **How to collect it:** Verified live: SAM.gov Get Opportunities Public API at https://api.sam.gov/opportunities/v2/search (free key from the Account Details page on sam.gov; parameters confirmed in the GSA API docs at open.gsa.gov/api/get-opportunities-public-api/ — api_key, postedFrom/postedTo in MM/dd/yyyy with a max 1-year span, ncode for NAICS, ptype for notice type, deptname/organizationName for agency, plus state/zip/classification-code filters, limit up to 1000 with offset). Poll daily with ncode=531120 and deptname scoped to General Services Administration; also sweep ptype=r (sources sought) and s (special notice), since regional PBS offices and their outsourced brokers post requirements inconsistently across notice types. Lease inventory: the IOLP leases workbook on inventory.data.gov (confirmed live, e.g. .../download/2026-8-7-iolp-leases.xlsx, dataset last updated 2026-08-11, accrual periodicity R/P1W) plus GSA's monthly regional lease inventory Excel files (gsa.gov confirms these are posted "after the 1st of each month" and include lease numbers, locations and rental rates). Awards: FPDS-NG ATOM feed / SAM Data Bank filtered to PSC X1AA. Entity resolution: GSA leases are signed with SPE LLCs, so map lessor-of-record names to REIT parents using 10-K Schedule III, investor supplements, and county mortgage records. Geocoding the delineated area is the hard part and the moat — it is free text ("bounded on the north by I-70, east by Broadway...") that must be parsed into a polygon and tested against the incumbent's lat/long. Optional fourth feed: House Transportation & Infrastructure and Senate EPW published public-buildings prospectus resolutions, which authorize above-threshold leases 1-3 years before award.
- **The bet / trade:** Primary: short Easterly Government Properties (NYSE: DEA) common equity, hedged as a pair against COPT Defense (NYSE: CDP) or a diversified REIT basket, sized up when the tape shows a cluster of adverse recompetes (delineated area excluding the incumbent building, or SF requirement materially below the sitting lease) against DEA assets whose leases expire in the next 12-36 months. Symmetric long side: cover/flip long when the tape shows sole-source succeeding-lease intent notices or FPDS awards back to the incumbent SPE. Holding horizon 6-18 months — you enter on the RLP, exit on the award notice or on the REIT's own 8-K/earnings admission. Secondary expressions: short Office Properties Income Trust (OPI) unsecured paper on federal-tenant roll, and short the junior tranches of single-asset/single-borrower CMBS collateralized by a GSA-leased building whose requirement has been advertised outside its delineated area.
- **Mechanism:** A GSA-leased building is a bond whose coupon is one tenant. Federal leasing procedure forces GSA to reveal its intent in public long before the landlord's investors hear it: to replace or renew space, the contracting officer must first advertise a requirement, and that advertisement fixes the geographic box and the size. If the delineated area excludes the incumbent's address, the incumbent is procedurally barred from bidding — the vacancy is already decided, 1-3 years before it happens. If the SF requirement is 40% below the sitting lease, the downsize is already decided. For a single-tenant, purpose-built federal building (a courthouse annex, an FBI field office, a VA outpatient clinic), losing the tenant is not a re-leasing problem, it is a near-total asset writedown, because the improvements are agency-specific and the market of substitute tenants is one buyer wide. The chain is therefore: RLP posted → delineated-area/SF test → probability of incumbent retention repriced → expected ABR loss and asset impairment → dividend coverage and NAV → equity. Nothing about this requires a forecast; it requires reading a procurement notice correctly and knowing where the building is.
- **Who's on the other side:** Long-only REIT income buyers and the small sell-side coverage group who underwrite DEA on a blanket "mission-critical, ~98% renewal" assumption applied uniformly across the portfolio, because management gives renewal guidance in aggregate and names specific at-risk assets only after the fact. They are slow for structural reasons, not lazy ones: GSA does not press-release recompetes; the notices are unstructured free text with no field for "delineated area"; SAM.gov's search is genuinely hostile to systematic reading; PBS regions and their broker contractors post under inconsistent notice types and sometimes advertise via their own broker sites first; and the lessor of record is an anonymous SPE LLC, so even someone reading every notice would not know a REIT was affected without a separate entity-resolution step. On the other side of the CMBS expression, servicers and B-piece buyers are working from remittance-report tape that only shows a problem once the tenant has already left and DSCR has broken.
- **Information lead:** 12-36 months versus the REIT's own disclosure. The advertisement is the first public act in a procurement that runs roughly two to three years from requirement development to occupancy; the REIT typically quantifies the loss only when the lease actually rolls, or at best when negotiations fail. Even the fallback signal — the FPDS award to a competing lessor — leads the incumbent's earnings-call admission by one to two quarters. Against the CMBS remittance tape the lead is larger still, since remittance only reflects the vacancy after it produces a coverage or delinquency event.
- **Why still under-exploited:** The 2025-26 attention on federal real estate went almost entirely to lease *terminations* — the publicized cancellation lists — which are the small, loud channel. The recompete pipeline is the large, quiet one, an order of magnitude bigger in dollar terms, and it grinds continuously rather than arriving as a headline. No vendor sells it: CoStar and the CRE data majors do not model federal procurement, the govcon data vendors (GovWin, HigherGov, USAspending wrappers) index solicitations for people trying to *win* contracts and have no reason to reverse the question into "which landlord loses," and none of them geocode delineated areas or resolve lessor SPEs to public issuers. Building it requires an odd stack of skills — GSA Leasing Desk Guide procedure, NLP over boundary prose, GIS, and REIT entity resolution — that rarely sits in one team. The feeds themselves are free and sanctioned public procurement and property records; the scarcity is entirely in the join.
- **Rough +EV:** DEA carries roughly 100 assets with a ~10-year weighted average lease term, so on the order of 8-12 leases enter the recompete window annually; add OPI's federal roll and a tail of GSA-collateralized SASB deals and the tape should throw 5-15 actionable, building-specific signals a year, of which perhaps 3-6 are materially adverse (adverse delineated area or >25% downsize on an asset worth 2-5% of ABR). Historically, disclosure of a single large federal non-renewal has moved DEA 4-8% in a session. Capturing half of that with 12-24 months of warning, on a pair-hedged book, is roughly 5-10% annualized alpha on the sleeve at a 0.4-0.7 Sharpe — attractive per dollar but capacity-constrained to maybe $20-40M given DEA's float and daily volume. The long side (confirmed succeeding-lease awards into a stock priced for federal attrition) is probably the better-compensated half and is where I would expect most of the realized P&L.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty: GSA's current operating posture. Under budget pressure and a shrinking federal footprint, agencies increasingly take short-term extensions or hold over rather than run a full competition — which means many expirations generate no advertisement at all, and absence of signal becomes ambiguous rather than bullish. A related risk is completeness: some regions and broker contractors surface requirements outside SAM.gov's standard notice types first, so the feed has holes you cannot easily measure. Secondary risk is that DEA is small, crowded on the short side, and already discounts federal attrition in aggregate — so the edge lives entirely in specificity (which building, which quarter, how much), and a book that only expresses "federal leases are bad" will get its face ripped off on the first sole-source renewal wave.

---
## 56. The First-Blade Tape

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Homebuilders & residential construction. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** NPDES construction stormwater general-permit coverage records — the Notice of Intent (NOI) effective date and Notice of Termination (NOT) date for every residential land-disturbance site in the US, now consolidated nationally in EPA's ICIS-NPDES as "General Permit Covered Facility" (permit type GPC) records under SIC 1521 / NAICS 236115-236117. Under CWA 402, no operator may turn a blade of dirt on >=1 disturbed acre (or any acre inside a larger common plan) until coverage is effective, so the effective date is a legally-forced, dated, geolocated timestamp of "we are starting to build this community." The Dec 21, 2025 Phase 2 deadline of the NPDES eReporting Rule forced all 46 delegated states to push NOIs/NOTs electronically to EPA for the first time — this national tape is roughly 8 months old.
- **How to collect it:** Verified live, free, no auth. (1) ECHO CWA REST API: https://echodata.epa.gov/echo/cwa_rest_services.get_facilities?output=JSON&p_ptype=GPC&p_sic=1521&p_st=TX then get_download?qid=<qid>&qcolumns=1,2,5,46,54,55,56,57,58,59,236 — returns CWPName, SourceID (state permit no.), CWPIssueDate, CWPEffectiveDate, CWPTerminationDate, MasterExternalPermitNmbr, PermittingAgency, FacLat/FacLong, county FIPS. I ran it: 167,989 GPC records nationally under SIC 1521; querying p_fn="D R HORTON" returns 344 sites across AL/NC/MO/TX, and the effective-date histogram is 2021:23, 2022:21, 2023:28, 2024:43, 2025:33, 2026:99 — the 2026 spike is the eRule Phase 2 backfill going live. (2) Weekly bulk: https://echo.epa.gov/files/echodownloads/npdes_downloads.zip (301MB) — ICIS_PERMITS.csv (PERMIT_TYPE_CODE='GPC'), ICIS_FACILITIES.csv, ICIS_MASTER_GENERAL_PERMITS.csv, NPDES_SICS.csv, NPDES_PERM_FEATURE_COORDS.csv. (3) State portals for the two fields ICIS drops — disturbed acreage and permittee/operator name: TCEQ Water Quality General Permits search (www2.tceq.texas.gov/wq_dpa, TXR150000) joined to TCEQ Central Registry customer search; NC DEQ NCG010000 (NCC###### IDs); ADEM eFile (ALR1#####); MO DNR (MORA#####); GA EPD, AZ ADEQ AZPDES eNOI, CO CDPHE, FL DEP. (4) EPA NeT-CGP for federal-CGP jurisdictions. Join key: builder subsidiary entity list scraped from each 10-K Exhibit 21 ("Lennar Homes of Texas Land & Construction Ltd", "D.R. Horton - Texas Ltd", "Pulte Home Company LLC", "Meritage Homes of Arizona Inc"), fuzzy-matched to both facility name and state permittee name, plus lat/long geofencing to known community footprints.
- **The bet / trade:** Equity pair trades inside the homebuilder/land-developer complex, sized off net NOI-minus-NOT acreage per issuer over the trailing 2-3 quarters, held through 1-2 quarterly prints (3-6 months). Purest expression: long/short Forestar Group (FOR) — a pure-play residential lot developer whose entire P&L is the exact activity that generates these NOIs and whose stock lives and dies on "lots under development / lots delivered" guidance. Secondary: long the self-developing builder whose NOI tape is inflecting up vs. short the one whose tape has gone dark for 2+ quarters, among DHI, LEN, PHM, MTH, TMHC, LGIH, CCS, GRBK, TPH — with NVR as the natural hedge leg, since NVR buys finished lots via options and files almost no NOIs, making it a clean beta control. Macro overlay: aggregate national SIC-1521 NOI effective-date count as a 2-3 quarter leading indicator on single-family starts, expressed in ITB/XHB or 6-9 month call spreads.
- **Mechanism:** NOI effective date = mass grading begins on a new community. Grading/clearing runs 3-5 months, wet utilities and paving another 3-5, then final plat and lot delivery, then 4-7 months of vertical — so the first closing in that community lands 9-18 months after the NOI. Quarterly NOI acreage per issuer therefore maps to community count 4-6 quarters forward, and NOI count maps to future community openings. The NOT filing (final stabilization) marks a community closing out. Community count is the single largest term in every sell-side homebuilder model: orders = community count x monthly absorption. Builders guide community count only vaguely and only quarterly ("up mid-single digits"), and community-count guide-downs are the classic homebuilder de-rate catalyst. The NOI-minus-NOT tape is the net derivative of that number, published weekly, that nobody computes. It also catches the thing management admits last: when a builder quietly stops putting dirt in the ground, the NOI tape goes silent 2-3 quarters before the community-count guidance cut.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side homebuilder analysts build community count from management guidance plus field-survey data from Zonda/Metrostudy and John Burns — surveyors who physically drive subdivisions on a quarterly cycle, see a site only once dirt is visibly moving and a sign is up, publish with a lag, and sell mostly to builders and consultants rather than to public equity. Macro housing traders watch Census permits and starts, which are national/regional with no issuer attribution and come after land development. MBS and rates desks own the mortgage side and never touch land. The people who already mine these NOI databases every day are SWPPP inspection firms and erosion-control contractors using them as a sales-lead list — they have no financial position. State environmental agencies and EPA compliance staff have zero financial interest. The structural reason everyone is slow: the data is genuinely ugly — 46 state programs, ~30 permit-ID formats, project names instead of company names, no acreage in the federal rollup, and until December 2025 no national electronic feed existed at all.
- **Information lead:** 4-6 quarters ahead of the builder's own disclosure (they report a community when it is selling; you see it when the dozer arrives). 1-2 quarters ahead of Zonda/Metrostudy field surveys, since the NOI must be effective before any visible disturbance and their survey-to-publication cycle is quarterly. 6-12 months ahead of county vertical permits and Census starts for the same ground. Within-quarter, ECHO refreshes weekly and states post NOIs within days of coverage, so you get a weekly tape on a metric the market only sees four times a year with a ~5-week reporting lag.
- **Why still under-exploited:** No alt-data vendor sells this to equity investors. Dodge, ConstructConnect and BuildCentral sell building permits and project starts — vertical construction, which is downstream. Zonda and John Burns sell field-surveyed community counts — coincident, not leading. Stormwater NOIs sit in the environmental-compliance silo, consumed by erosion-control salespeople, and have never been mapped to public-company subsidiary entities. The national consolidation is brand new: the eRule Phase 2 deadline was Dec 21, 2025, and the empirical fingerprint is visible in the data I pulled — D.R. Horton-named GPC records with 2026 effective dates (99) triple the 2025 count (33), because states only just started pushing NOIs federally. The entity-resolution work (Exhibit 21 subsidiary lists x inconsistent state permittee strings x lat/long geofencing) is exactly the kind of unglamorous plumbing that keeps casual users out, which is the moat.
- **Rough +EV:** Low-frequency, high-payoff-at-turns rather than a quarterly nowcast machine. Community count flows roughly 1:1 into orders, and order surprises move homebuilder single names 4-8% on the print. Assume the tape gets you directionally right on ~60-65% of community-count guidance inflections vs. a 50% baseline, across ~8 names x 4 prints = ~32 events/year, capturing ~2.5% per correct pair and giving back ~2% per wrong one: roughly +0.3-0.6% per event, ~10-18% annualized gross on the sleeve before costs and financing. In practice only 2-5 high-conviction land-development inflections per year are clean — mostly at cycle turns — and those are worth 5-15% on the specific expression. The fat tail is Forestar-type names: FOR has moved >15% on lot-delivery guidance revisions, and 2-3 quarters of lead on its development pipeline is the single highest-payoff use of the tape.
- **Confidence:** Medium. Biggest uncertainty is entity attribution coverage: roughly 60-70% of large builders' lots now come from third-party developers and land banks, so the NOI operator is frequently a developer or land banker rather than the builder who will ultimately sell the homes — meaning the tape natively reads the land-development industry, not cleanly the builder. Compounding it, the federal ICIS rollup carries no disturbed-acreage field and populates permittee name inconsistently, so the fields that matter most require scraping uneven state portals, and small-site authorizations (1-5 acres in some states, including Texas) can be covered by a posted site notice with no NOI in the database at all. If acres-to-lots-to-specific-builder attribution proves too lossy, the signal degrades from builder-level pairs to a macro land-development indicator — still tradeable through FOR and ITB/XHB, but at materially lower alpha.

---
## 57. The Discard Pile

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Construction materials & aggregates. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** Regional seismic-network catalogs of **commercial quarry blasts** — the ~2,600/yr US events that seismologists label `quarry blast` / `explosion` / `mining explosion` specifically so they can be *deleted* from earthquake statistics. Each record carries origin time (to the second), lat/lon, and local magnitude (a monotone proxy for charge weight, i.e. tons of rock broken off the face). This is a direct physical measurement of the production act itself — the instant rock is separated from the deposit — at ~1-day latency, per-site, for free. Aggregates is the last major industrial commodity with **no high-frequency public volume series**: USGS Mineral Industry Surveys for crushed stone are quarterly with a 3–4 month lag, and VMC/MLM/KNF disclose shipment tons only at earnings, ~5–6 weeks after quarter end. Between prints, the entire sell side is modeling volumes off rainfall and DOT lettings. Verified live against the USGS FDSN event service: CY2025 = 1,539 `quarry blast` + 876 `explosion` + 239 `mining explosion`; **median origin→last-update latency 0.92 days** (p25 0.18d); continuous history back through at least 2010 (757–1,350/yr in the western US). The events are unmistakably industrial, not natural: **96% weekday** (3,504 Mon–Fri vs 134 Sat/Sun) and sharply peaked 10am–4pm local, max at noon–1pm. Density: 1,248 distinct ~2km sites over 19.5 months, **57 sites with ≥12 blasts (≥monthly cadence), 15 with ≥24**. Geography is concentrated: CA 1,316, OK 684, WA 500, OR 308, MT 273, NV 70, UT 48, ID 34, AK 171.
- **How to collect it:** Three components, all verified working today.

**1. Blast catalog (free, no API key).** `https://earthquake.usgs.gov/fdsnws/event/1/query?format=csv&starttime=YYYY-MM-DD&endtime=YYYY-MM-DD&eventtype=quarry%20blast` — repeat for `eventtype=explosion` and `eventtype=mining%20explosion` (the valid enum is exposed at `/fdsnws/event/1/application.json`). Cap is 20,000 events/request; `/count` endpoint for sizing. Poll daily. For deeper/earlier records go to the contributing networks directly: SCEDC (scedc.caltech.edu) and NCEDC (ncedc.org) for California, PNSN (pnsn.org) for WA/OR, Oklahoma Geological Survey, Montana Bureau of Mines & Geology, Univ. of Utah Seismograph Stations, Nevada Seismological Lab.

**2. Operator attribution — the join that makes it tradeable.** `https://arlweb.msha.gov/OpenGovernmentData/DataSets/Mines.zip` (7.3 MB, live, HTTP 200). Pipe-delimited, 59 columns, **47,589 geocoded mines**, with `LATITUDE`, `LONGITUDE`, `CURRENT_MINE_NAME`, `CURRENT_OPERATOR_NAME`, `CURRENT_CONTROLLER_NAME` (ultimate parent), `CURRENT_MINE_STATUS`, `CURRENT_STATUS_DT`. Nearest-neighbour within ~3 km. I ran this join on the top clusters and it resolves cleanly every time: 34.18/-117.92 → *Azusa Rock, controller **Vulcan Materials Company*** (2.7 km); 34.38/-96.84 → ***Martin Marietta** Mill Creek* (3.0 km); 37.92/-121.96 → *Clayton Aggregates, **Martin Marietta** Northern California* + *CEMEX Clayton*; 32.90/-116.94 → ***Martin Marietta** San Diego Aggregates, Lakeside–Vigilante* (0.7 km); 35.06/-118.34 → *Mojave Plant & Quarry, **CalPortland**, controller **Taiheiyo Cement*** + *Tehachapi Cement, **UNACEM***; 36.90/-121.62 → *A.R. Wilson Quarry, **Granite Rock Co***; 46.98/-122.22 → *Kapowsin Quarry, Washington Rock Quarries*. So a raw lat/lon becomes "this ticker, this asset, this commodity, this status."

**3. Coverage extension (the moat).** Pull continuous waveforms from EarthScope/IRIS DMC — `https://service.iris.edu/fdsnws/dataselect/1/` — with ObsPy and run a **matched-filter/template detector** using confirmed blasts as templates. Quarry blasts recur at nearly identical coordinates with nearly identical source mechanisms, so template matching is unusually powerful: it detects blasts far below the network's published review threshold, and it works in regions where the network never *labels* blasts at all (TexNet in Texas, CERI/New Madrid for TN-MO-AR, Georgia Tech's network, Lamont/NESN in the Northeast). This is how you extend past the labeled catalog into VMC/MLM's Southeast core.
- **The bet / trade:** **Primary:** directional position in **Martin Marietta (MLM)** and **Vulcan (VMC)**, entered 3–5 weeks before each quarterly report and held **through the print** (the event where the asymmetry resolves). Direction and size set by the controller-attributed West/Southwest blast index (CA + OK + NV + AZ + OR clusters, energy-weighted via magnitude, not just counted) versus consensus aggregate shipment-volume growth. Express as equity, or as short-dated (~30–45 day) options capturing the earnings move when the divergence is >1.5σ. 4 prints/yr × 2 names.

**Pair overlay:** long/short **MLM vs. VMC**. The catalog reads *both* companies' California assets simultaneously and independently (Azusa Rock/CalMat Reliance = Vulcan; Clayton + Lakeside–Vigilante = Martin Marietta), so the pair strips out the unobserved Southeast and isolates the West-region divergence — precisely where the two most differ after MLM absorbed Lehigh's West region.

**Secondary, higher-conviction discrete trade — "the silent quarry."** A 30+ day blast gap at a site with ≥18 months of ≥2/month cadence, cross-checked against `CURRENT_MINE_STATUS` flipping to *Intermittent*, is a loud, individually-verifiable stoppage: permit loss, litigation, reserve exhaustion, weather shutdown, or demand collapse. **Blast absence carries more information than blast presence.** For cement specifically, the Mojave (CalPortland/Taiheiyo 5233.T) and Tehachapi (UNACEM) clusters are kiln limestone feed — a gap there is a **California kiln-outage detector** in the tightest, highest-priced cement market in the US, readable months before it appears in any disclosure. Smaller, geography-matched names — **Knife River (KNF)**, whose MT/OR/WA/ID/CA/AK footprint overlaps the covered region almost exactly, and **Granite Construction (GVA)** — offer the best signal-to-market-cap ratio.
- **Mechanism:** Rock cannot be sold until it is broken, and it is broken by blasting. A quarry drills and shoots a bench that yields roughly 2–8 weeks of crusher feed, so the blast physically precedes the shipment. More importantly, blasting is a **forward-looking management commitment**, not a passive activity trace: drilling and loading a face is expensive, irreversible, permit-constrained, and scheduled against *expected* demand. Operators shoot ahead of anticipated orders and stop shooting when stockpiles build. So blast cadence reveals what the plant manager believes about the next quarter, weeks before the ton is invoiced and months before it is disclosed.

The magnitude field converts count into quantity: local magnitude scales with the logarithm of charge weight, and charge weight is roughly proportional to tons of rock fragmented (quarry powder factors are stable per-site). Energy-summing per site per month yields a tons-broken estimate rather than a mere event count. Because each site's geology, bench height, and powder factor are fixed, the *within-site* time series is far cleaner than the cross-site comparison — you difference each quarry against its own history.

The chain: blast cadence/energy at attributed sites → crusher feed → shipment tons → segment revenue and, through fixed-cost absorption, segment margin → the aggregates volume line that drives the stock reaction at the print. And in a business where incremental margins are dominated by operating leverage over a fixed plant, a volume surprise is amplified into an outsized earnings surprise.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side building-materials analysts and long-only infrastructure/industrials funds, who model aggregate volumes from a well-worn and entirely *backward-looking* kit: NOAA precipitation and heating-degree-days, state DOT letting volumes, Dodge/ConstructConnect starts, ACS put-in-place, and management's own guidance. Every one of those is a proxy for whether shipping was *possible* or whether demand *should* exist. None of them observes whether the operator actually broke rock. Weather tells you it rained in Sacramento; the blast catalog tells you Clayton Aggregates shot four benches anyway.

They are slow for a structural reason rather than a lazy one: the data is not in any vendor's catalog, is not tagged as economic data anywhere, and is unfindable unless you already hold the domain model that says "the seismology community records industrial explosions." Quant shops screen vendor catalogs and alternative-data marketplaces; this sits in an FDSN geophysics API whose entire purpose is earthquake hazard. It requires a seismologist's instinct plus a mining-regulatory join to even locate, and then a second, harder step — matched-filter waveform detection — to extend beyond the published labels. Discretionary fundamental investors have the domain knowledge but not the geophysics; quants have the pipelines but not the reason to look. The intersection is close to empty.
- **Information lead:** Two stacked leads.

**Disclosure lead (the big one): 6 weeks to 5 months.** MLM and VMC report shipment tons only quarterly, roughly 5–6 weeks after quarter end. Blasts land in the catalog at a **verified median 0.92 days** from origin (p25 0.18 days). So a blast on the first business day of a quarter is legible to you ~4.5 months before that quarter's volumes are disclosed, and the full-quarter index is complete ~6 weeks before the print. The pre-earnings window is the entire quarter, not a sliver.

**Physical lead (the subtler one): 3–8 weeks.** Blast → crush → ship means the broken rock in month *t* is largely the revenue of months *t* to *t+2*. Because operators shoot against forecast demand rather than realized demand, the series leads even the company's own internal shipment data — it is closer to an order-book signal than a production signal.

**Discrete-event lead: 3–5 months.** A quarry going silent, or a cement plant's feed quarry stopping, is visible within ~30–45 days of the last blast. That same fact reaches the market only via the earnings call, a permit-hearing news story, or an MSHA status change filed later — and for a private operator like CalPortland or a foreign parent like Taiheiyo/UNACEM, it may never be disclosed at all, reaching the market only indirectly as regional cement tightness and price.
- **Why still under-exploited:** The reason is almost poetic and it is the whole thesis: **this dataset exists only because seismologists label quarry blasts in order to throw them away.** Networks identify and tag commercial explosions so they can be *excluded* from earthquake catalogs and hazard models. The economic signal is the waste stream of a discipline that has no interest in it. Nobody curates it, nobody markets it, and its custodians actively regard it as contamination.

Concretely, it clears every "is this already a product" test. It is not in Kpler, Vortexa, YipitData, M Science, Ortex, Orbital Insight, SpaceKnow, StreetLight, or any alt-data marketplace. There is no "quarry blast index." The academic literature on quarry-blast discrimination is real but is entirely about *seismic hazard and nuclear test-ban monitoring* — its explicit purpose is removing these events, and it never crosses into commodity or equity analysis. USGS's own aggregates statistics (Mineral Industry Surveys) are survey-based, quarterly, and lagged, and USGS has never connected its earthquake catalog to its minerals division.

The friction is genuine and durable, which is why it stays underexploited: it demands three unrelated competencies stacked together — FDSN geophysics APIs, mining regulatory data (the MSHA controller hierarchy), and building-materials equity analysis — plus a geospatial join at ~2 km resolution and, for full coverage, custom matched-filter detection on raw waveforms. That is a multi-week build for someone who already knows all three domains, and the payoff is invisible until the join is done. The starting hints for this domain point at MSHA *production* reports; the real value in MSHA turns out to be something else entirely — it is the **geocoded operator/controller registry** that converts an anonymous seismic coordinate into a ticker.
- **Rough +EV:** **Honest and bounded.** Attributable aggregate/cement sites with usable cadence number roughly 30–45 after filtering out the non-construction blasters that dominate the raw counts (Rio Tinto's Boron borate mine at ~137 events, Montana Resources' Butte copper pit at ~97, and military ordnance near the Chocolate Mountains range at ~85 — all must be excluded, and all are trivially excluded by the MSHA commodity join). Those 30–45 sites represent perhaps **5–12% of MLM's or VMC's company-wide tons** — enough to drive a confident *regional* read, not enough to call company EPS on its own. This is a high-quality input to a volume model, not an oracle, and anyone claiming otherwise is overfitting.

**Sizing.** ~8 earnings events/yr across MLM/VMC (plus KNF/GVA if traded), of which realistically **3–5 per year** show a >1.5σ divergence between the blast index and the weather/lettings-implied consensus baseline — those are the only ones worth taking. Assume a **57–62% directional hit rate** on the West-region volume surprise versus a 50% coin flip. On an equity expression that is roughly **+150 to +350 bp per event** over a 2–4 week hold, i.e. low-to-mid single digits annualized on capital deployed — respectable but unspectacular. Short-dated options on the earnings move geared to the same edge push this to a low-double-digit IRR on the sleeve, with correspondingly fatter tails.

**Where the real convexity is: the silent-quarry detector**, ~3–8 firings/yr. A confirmed multi-week stoppage at a top-10 US quarry or at a California cement plant's feed quarry is a genuinely non-consensus, binary, months-early fact with an asymmetric payoff — the sort of event that moves a mid-cap 8–15% and is currently discovered by the market only at the print. Backtestable over **2010–2026, ~64 quarters** of catalog history, which is thin for a formal Sharpe estimate but adequate to establish whether the blast-to-shipment relationship is stable and to calibrate the divergence threshold.
- **Confidence:** **Medium.**

**Single biggest uncertainty: cataloging-policy drift.** Blast counts are a joint function of production *and* of the network's willingness and ability to detect, review, and publish these events — and the second term is unstable in exactly the way that ruins naive signals. New stations, retired stations, a switch to an automated location pipeline, a change in analyst review practice, or a shift in the magnitude-of-completeness threshold will all move blast counts for reasons that have nothing to do with a single ton of rock. This is aggravated by the fact that networks label blasts as housekeeping, not as a product, so there is no stability guarantee, no versioning, and no change log. The mitigation is mandatory, not optional: normalize every site series against (a) station uptime and network geometry pulled from the FDSN **station** service, and (b) a control series of small natural earthquakes in the same region and magnitude band. If a "production collapse" and the control series move together, it was instrumentation. Skipping this step guarantees the signal is dominated by seismometers rather than by quarries.

**Second, and structurally limiting: geographic coverage.** The labeled catalog is dense in CA, OK, WA, OR, NV, UT, MT, AK and effectively absent in the Southeast and Texas — Georgia returned 1 event and Tennessee 1 over 19.5 months — which is precisely where VMC's and MLM's core volumes sit. Until the matched-filter waveform extension is built and validated against TexNet, CERI, and the Georgia Tech network, this reads the West and only the West, and the mega-cap application stays partial. That extension is the difference between an interesting regional input and a genuine company-level edge, and it is unproven until someone builds it.

**Third, minor:** magnitude-to-tonnage conversion is site-specific and noisy in the cross-section; trust within-site differences, distrust cross-site levels.

---
## 58. The Restart Flare

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Fertilizer & crop inputs. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** NASA FIRMS near-real-time VIIRS 375m active-fire detections (Suomi-NPP + NOAA-20 + NOAA-21), geofenced to the individual flare stacks of the world's ~120 export-relevant ammonia/urea complexes — used to detect the synthesis-loop blowdown flare when a plant trips, and, more importantly, the startup flare when it comes back. Corroborated with NOAA/Colorado School of Mines VIIRS Nightfire (VNF), which reports per-source temperature (400K to >2500K) and source area, letting you separate a 1800-2000K emergency depressurization from a pilot flame. The unit of analysis is not "is there a fire" but the flare time series per stack: big flare = trip; second big flare followed by return to pilot-only baseline = plant is physically back online.
- **How to collect it:** Free MAP_KEY at firms.modaps.eosdis.nasa.gov. Pull via the Area API: https://firms.modaps.eosdis.nasa.gov/api/area/csv/[MAP_KEY]/VIIRS_NOAA20_NRT/[west,south,east,north]/[day_range] — verified live: URT delivers in <60 seconds over North America, NRT within 60 minutes globally, limit 5,000 transactions per 10 minutes (ample to poll 120 geofences every 15 min). Backfill and corroborate with nightly VNF CSVs at eogdata.mines.edu/wwwdata/viirs_products/vnf/ (free license agreement required since Jan 2025; history to 2012 — this is what you backtest on). The hard, unautomatable part: hand-build the geofence library. You must locate the FLARE STACK, not the plant centroid, to ~500m using Google Earth / Sentinel-2 imagery, for each complex. Highest-value nodes are where Western price reporters are thinnest: Egypt (Abu Qir, MOPCO/Damietta, Helwan, NCIC Ain Sokhna, Alexfert), Trinidad's Point Lisas estate (Nutrien ~2.2Mtpa across three plants, Yara ~1.3Mtpa across three, plus Tringen/Proman), Algeria (Sorfert Arzew, AOA), Nigeria (Indorama Eleme, Dangote), Iran (Pardis, Kermanshah), and the cleanly isolated US Midwest plants (CF Port Neal, Iowa Fertilizer Wever, Koch Enid and Dodge City, CF Verdigris/Woodward/Yazoo City, LSB El Dorado/Pryor). Total data cost: $0.
- **The bet / trade:** Two legs on CBOT cash-settled fertilizer futures, chosen to match the basis of the affected plant: UFE (Urea Granular FOB Egypt), UME (FOB Middle East), UFV (FOB US Gulf), UFB (CFR Brazil). LEG 1 (trip, long): on detection of a large unreported flare at an export-relevant complex, buy the front two contract months. Hold 1-4 weeks. LEG 2 (restart, short — the differentiated leg): on detection of the startup flare signature while the trade press still carries the plant as "offline, no restart date," sell the front month and hold to expiry. Horizon is set by the contract mechanics: these settle to the arithmetic average of the WEEKLY ICIS and Profercy midpoint assessments across the whole contract month, so a single early-month physical read prices three or four subsequent weekly prints. Liquid overflow expressions for capacity: long CF Industries (CF) 1-2 month calls when the ex-North-America offline index spikes (CF is a net beneficiary of competitors' downtime); LSB Industries (LXU) for US-specific outages; Abu Qir (ABUK) / MOPCO (MFPC) on the EGX for Egyptian gas curtailments.
- **Mechanism:** Ammonia synthesis runs a closed loop at 150-200 bar. Any trip — compressor, reformer, power dip, or a government gas curtailment — forces depressurization through relief valves to the flare: a brief, enormous, ~1800-2000K event that VIIRS resolves in SWIR/MWIR. Restart is the mirror image: hours to days of off-spec syngas and ammonia routed to flare while the loop repressurizes and catalyst comes to temperature. Steady-state operation emits only a pilot, typically below threshold. So the flare series brackets the outage window with physical certainty. Ammonia is the feedstock for urea, UAN, and (with sulphur) DAP/MAP; nitrogen runs at high utilization with thin spare capacity, so a single 1.0-1.5 Mtpa complex down three weeks removes a real slice of the exportable ton. Egypt and the Arab Gulf are the marginal export tons into Europe and Brazil, so curtailments there propagate straight into the UFE/UME assessments. The payoff link is unusually mechanical: CME states in its own FAQ that final settlement is the average of midpoint assessments "published weekly" by ICIS and Profercy over the contract month. The settlement input is literally a lagging, relationship-sourced, once-a-week human judgment. A sensor that reports plant state in under an hour, several times inside one contract month, is arithmetic alpha on that settlement.
- **Who's on the other side:** Physical nitrogen traders and producer hedge desks, who source outage news from broker chatter, plant contacts, and PRA correspondents — real information, but human, relationship-gated, and structurally conflicted. Producers are slow and vague about their own downtime by design: a confirmed outage moves price against their unsold book, and a confirmed restart moves it against their sold book, so neither event generates a press release. Also on the other side: the ICIS and Profercy correspondents themselves, who publish weekly and print only what they can source and confirm — their incentive is accuracy, not speed, and they are paid to be right, not early. And momentum/CTA flow in the urea contracts with no fundamental view at all. The structural reason they stay slow: nobody in the chain has a business model that rewards knowing about a RESTART five days early. Producers won't say, buyers can't know, the PRA can't confirm it. The satellite has no book and no relationships to protect.
- **Information lead:** Trip detection: ~1-12 hours from event to signal (URT/NRT plus next overpass; three satellites give multiple looks per night). Trade press carries an unconfirmed report in 1-4 days for well-covered US/European plants, 3-10 days for Egypt, Algeria, Nigeria, Iran, Trinidad. The price-relevant weekly assessment moves at the next print. Net lead on the thing that actually settles the contract: 2-8 days. Restart detection is the larger edge: startup flares are visible the night they occur, while the trade press routinely carries a plant as "offline, no restart date given" for 1-3 weeks after it is physically running, precisely because restarts are announced by no one. Net lead: 5-20 days. Versus company disclosure (quarterly production volumes), the lead is weeks to a full quarter.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Satellite flare monitoring is thoroughly commercialized — for oil and gas. World Bank GGFR, Capterio, Kayrros and the whole flaring-intensity ESG complex have built geofence libraries, tooling, and analyst attention pointed at upstream and midstream hydrocarbons. Essentially nobody has built the equivalent library for ammonia and urea stacks, because nitrogen is a small, clubby, physically-traded market whose participants buy information through relationships rather than screens. The nitrogen research industry (Profercy, Argus, CRU, Green Markets, Fertecon) is a human-correspondent business — it literally sells the lag, so it has no incentive to disintermediate itself with a sensor. And the entry cost is the right kind of ugly: you must hand-locate ~120 flare stacks at sub-kilometer precision from imagery and then learn each stack's idiosyncratic signature, which is weeks of tedious manual labor with no shortcut and no vendor to buy it from. That is exactly the profile of something that stays unexploited. The highest-value nodes are also the ones where Western price reporters have the thinnest human coverage (Egypt, Algeria, Iran, Nigeria, Trinidad) and where the satellite has no coverage gap whatsoever. Egypt's confirmed gas production decline post-Zohr, summer blackouts, and shift to LNG imports make industrial curtailment a recurring seasonal event — and CME lists a dedicated Egyptian urea contract (UFE) against it.
- **Rough +EV:** Frequency: across ~120 monitored complexes, unplanned trips exceeding 5 days at export-relevant plants run roughly 30-60/year globally; perhaps 15-25/year are at plants large and export-relevant enough to justify a trade — call it 1-2 actionable signals per month, clustered in Egyptian gas-curtailment season (June-Sept) and US Gulf hurricane/turnaround season. Edge size: a 1+ Mtpa complex down 3+ weeks has historically moved the relevant FOB urea assessment 3-8% across the following two to four weekly prints. Capturing a third of that ahead of the print, on a contract that averages weekly assessments over the month, is ~1-3% of notional per event on the trip leg; the restart-fade leg is smaller (~1-2%) but higher hit-rate because you are fading a stale, crowded consensus rather than predicting news. Blended: ~15-25 trades/year at ~1.5% average gross edge, hit rate plausibly 58-65%. Honest deductions: roughly half of detected flares will be planned turnarounds already on published outage calendars or contamination from neighboring units, and must be netted out, which caps the tradeable set. UFE/UME are genuinely illiquid — thin open interest, wide screens, mostly broker-negotiated blocks — so realistic capacity is $5-40M, not $500M. Net of transaction costs, a plausible target is Sharpe 0.8-1.4 on deployed capital, with CF/LXU/ABUK equity expressions available to add capacity at lower signal purity.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The biggest uncertainty is detection SPECIFICITY and false-negative rate, not whether the data exists — I verified the feed, the latency, the API limits, the contract codes, and the settlement mechanic directly. What I have NOT verified empirically is what fraction of modern ammonia plants route trip gas to an elevated flare large enough to clear the VIIRS 375m threshold, versus venting, recovering purge gas to fuel, or using an enclosed ground flare with a suppressed thermal signature. Enclosed ground flares are increasingly standard on newer builds and are much harder to see from orbit; if 40% of trips are radiometrically invisible, signal frequency drops enough to break the economics. Second uncertainty: geofence contamination at co-located sites — Point Lisas, the Mississippi chemical corridor, Ain Sokhna — where refineries and methanol plants sit within a kilometer of the ammonia flare; solvable with tighter geofences and per-stack signature learning, but at a cost in precision. The disciplined first step is a cheap, capital-free backtest: pull 3 years of free VNF history over 20 plants whose outage and restart dates are already known from public trade press and company disclosure, and measure hit rate, false-negative rate, and realized lead time before risking a dollar.

---
## 59. The Ironville Meter

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Steel & aluminum. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** FERC-mandated daily point-level "Total Scheduled Quantity" on interstate natural gas pipeline Informational Postings (EBBs), filtered to the delivery points that are named for specific steel and aluminum plants. Flagship instance, verified live: Generation Pipeline, LLC (Enbridge, FERC-jurisdictional, ~25 miles, Toledo OH) has exactly SEVEN flow points, and one of them is literally named "CLEVELAND CLIFFS" — location ID GP103, lat 41.682808 / lon -83.492098, milepost 23.418 — which is the Cleveland-Cliffs Toledo HBI (hot-briquetted iron) plant in the Ironville industrial district. Because the pipeline has only 3 real delivery meters (GP101 OREGON CLEAN ENERGY PLANT, GP102 INTERSTATE CHEMICAL, GP103 CLEVELAND CLIFFS) fed from 2 receipts (GP001 PEPL MAUMEE, GP002 ANR FALLEN TIMBERS), GP103's scheduled quantity is an unblended, unambiguous DAILY GAS METER on a single 1.9 Mt/yr HBI plant. Same technique verified on other systems: East Tennessee Natural Gas carries "ALCOA NORTH LATERAL", "ALCOA SOUTH LATERAL" and "SMELTER SERVICE CORPORATION" (secondary aluminum); Texas Eastern carries "AK STEEL - WARREN CO., OH" and "ALCOA-LAVACA PIPELINE - CALHOUN, TX". The panel generalizes to ~20-40 named metals meters across ~40 interstate pipelines.
- **How to collect it:** Three free, no-login, sanctioned pulls, all confirmed reachable: (1) POINT GEOGRAPHY / NAME CROSSWALK — Enbridge LINK publishes a raw JSON blob per pipeline at https://infopost.enbridge.com/LocationMap/{BU}/bu-all.js with fields primaryFlowLocationId, primaryFlowLocationDescription, latitude, longitude, milePost. BU codes: GPL (Generation Pipeline), TE (Texas Eastern, 921 pts), ET (East Tennessee, 259 pts), AG (Algonquin), NXUS (NEXUS), SESH, STT, MNUS. One curl each; grep the descriptions for steel/aluminum operators, then confirm identity by reverse-geocoding the lat/lon onto the plant footprint. (2) DAILY FLOW — the NAESB WGQ "Operationally Available Capacity" dataset that 18 CFR 284.13(d) forces every interstate pipeline to post publicly for every nomination cycle. It carries, per location: Design Capacity, Operating Capacity, TOTAL SCHEDULED QUANTITY, Operationally Available Capacity. Cycles are Timely (~13:00 CT day-ahead), Evening (~18:00), Intraday 1/2/3 (~10:00, 14:30, 19:00), each posted within ~1 hour. Kinder Morgan's EBB is the cleanest scrape and is wide open: https://pipeline2.kindermorgan.com/Capacity/OpAvailPoint.aspx?code=TGP (also NGPL, SNG, EPNG, CIG, KMLP, MEP, EEC) with EXCEL/DOCX/PDF export buttons and a companion https://pipeline2.kindermorgan.com/LocationDataDownload/LocDataDwnld.aspx?code=TGP. Enbridge LINK at https://infopost.enbridge.com/InfoPost/, Boardwalk GasQuest at https://www.gasquest.com/informational-posting (Texas Gas Transmission, Gulf South — the western-Kentucky/Louisiana systems), Energy Transfer iPost (Panhandle Eastern, Trunkline, Rover), TC Energy EBB (ANR, Columbia Gas, Great Lakes), Williams 1Line (Transco). (3) ATTRIBUTION KEY — the quarterly FERC "Index of Customers" on the same EBBs (e.g. https://pipeline2.kindermorgan.com/IndexOfCust/IOC.aspx?code=TGP) names the actual shipper and contract MDQ per point, so you can prove a point belongs to Cleveland-Cliffs Steel LLC / Nucor / SDI rather than guessing from a free-text label. Also pull each pipeline's Critical Notices and Planned Service Outage postings to suppress false positives when the PIPE, not the PLANT, is the thing that went down. Engineering effort: roughly two weeks to build the crosswalk plus a nightly scraper; storage is trivial. Everything above is data the pipelines are legally required to publish to the general public.
- **The bet / trade:** Primary: directional CLF (Cleveland-Cliffs) equity and short-dated listed options, sized into the quarterly print. Trigger: cumulative GP103 scheduled Dth over the quarter, converted to HBI tons at ~10.5 GJ/t (full rate ≈ 50-55 MDth/d for a 1.9 Mt/yr plant), diverging more than ~15% from the run-rate implied by company guidance. If Toledo runs materially cold or goes to zero for >10 consecutive gas days with no 8-K, buy puts/short into the print; if it restarts hard off an idle, buy calls. Holding horizon 2-10 weeks, exit on the earnings print or on the confirming 8-K, whichever comes first. Secondary/confirming leg in the metallics complex: on a confirmed unannounced Toledo outage of >3 weeks, go long front/second-month CME US Midwest #1 Busheling Ferrous Scrap futures (BUS) and/or CME US Midwest HRC (CRU) futures, 1-3 month horizon — HBI is the marginal substitute for prime scrap in EAF flat-roll melts and the US is structurally short prime. Panel extension using the same meter technique: STLD (Columbus MS aluminum flat-roll ramp), X (Big River 2 ramp), NUE (Louisiana DRI outages), and CME Midwest Aluminum Premium (AUP) off secondary-smelter melt rates such as the ETNG "SMELTER SERVICE CORPORATION" point.
- **Mechanism:** A gas-based DRI/HBI shaft furnace is a near-pure natural gas machine: Midrex/Energiron consume roughly 9.5-10.5 GJ per tonne of product, and essentially all of that gas arrives through one metered pipeline tap. Gas burn is therefore close to LINEAR in tons produced, with almost no confounding load — unlike a steel mill's electricity, which is shared with an ISO, or a blast furnace, which has coke inventory buffering weeks of output. Nominations are made by the shipper the day before for a specific gas day, so the meter leads physical production by a day and leads any disclosure by weeks. Chain: (1) plant management decides to idle, derate, or restart; (2) the very next nomination cycle reflects it, because nobody pays for gas they will not burn; (3) the pipeline is legally obliged to post the scheduled quantity for that point within about an hour; (4) tons of HBI move, which changes Cliffs' metallics cost position, its "other businesses" volumes, and the merchant HBI/prime-scrap balance; (5) weeks or a full quarter later, the company tells the market. On the price side, HBI/DRI is the swing substitute for #1 busheling; ~1.9 Mt/yr of Toledo HBI is roughly 160 kt/month of prime-scrap-equivalent, on the order of 5% of US monthly prime availability, so a multi-week outage is a real tightening impulse into the monthly AMM busheling settle, which is exactly what the CME BUS contract settles against.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side steel analysts and long-only materials PMs who build Cliffs' cost-per-ton and metallics volumes off management guidance and off AISI weekly capability utilization — a national aggregate for RAW STEEL that does not cover HBI/DRI at all and never identifies a plant. Generalist high-beta traders in CLF, which is one of the most retail-heavy, highest-short-interest names in US materials, where operational headlines routinely move the stock 4-10% in a day. Also the passive metals ETF complex, which prices nothing at facility level. The subtle part: natural gas fundamental desks and their vendors (Criterion Research, Wood Mackenzie/Genscape, S&P PointLogic, LSEG) DO ingest this exact file every single day — but their entity resolution stops at "industrial demand, Bcf/d, by region," because they are selling gas balances into Henry Hub, not equity signals. They see GP103 go to zero and record it as 50 MDth/d of lost Midwest industrial demand. Nobody on that desk owns CLF, and nobody on the CLF desk has ever opened an EBB. The edge is a pure cross-asset-class information silo, not a data-access advantage.
- **Information lead:** Versus the company: a nomination change is visible the day BEFORE the gas flows, and Cliffs discloses plant idlings via 8-K/press release only when it chooses to — historically days to several weeks after the fact — and discloses HBI volumes and cost impact only at the quarterly print. So the lead is roughly 1-4 weeks against an 8-K and up to a full 13-week quarter against the earnings model. Versus any industry data: AISI weekly production is national/regional raw steel with a ~4-day lag and excludes HBI entirely; USGS and AIST plant-level metallics data are annual; Fastmarkets/AMM HBI and busheling assessments are monthly and are themselves built by phoning the same market that does not yet know. Versus the vendors who technically hold the data: zero timing lead, but a total interpretation lead, since they never map the point to the equity. Practical realized lead in the panel: 2 to 8 weeks of unambiguous, plant-specific operating-rate knowledge before the metals market prices it.
- **Why still under-exploited:** Four independent frictions keep it unexploited. (1) Wrong-market siloing: the file is a commodity in natural gas and unheard of in metals; the vendors who resell it aggregate away exactly the facility identity that makes it valuable. (2) Nobody sells the product: there is no commercial "steel and aluminum plant operating rate from gas nominations" feed anywhere — Kpler/Vortexa cover seaborne cargoes, YipitData/M Science cover consumer transactions, Ortex covers borrow. Building this requires deciding to look at a gas EBB while thinking about a steel stock, which is a genuinely unusual cognitive jump. (3) The crosswalk is brutal manual work: point names are unstandardized free text that drifts ("CLEVELAND CLIFFS", "AK STEEL - WARREN CO., OH", "ALCOA-LAVACA PIPELINE - CALHOUN, TX  (DE" — truncated mid-string in the source data), each of ~40 pipelines has its own ASP.NET postback grid or SPA with a different export path, and you must join name + lat/lon + FERC Index of Customers to prove attribution. There is no join key; you build it by hand once. (4) The specific asset is invisible: Generation Pipeline, LLC is a 25-mile orphan interstate that essentially nobody in finance has ever heard of, buried as one of 25 business units on Enbridge's LINK postings site — and because it has only seven total points, it is the cleanest single-plant meter in American heavy industry, purely by accident of its size.
- **Rough +EV:** Single-meter version (GP103/CLF): the Toledo HBI plant has produced roughly 4-6 actionable regime changes per year since commissioning — full idlings, extended maintenance turns, and sustained derates. CLF moves 4-10% on operational news. Assuming a 58-63% directional hit rate front-running disclosure, expect ~1.5-3% expected return per episode on a delta-1 position, or 4-9% per episode on 2-6 week options with correspondingly fatter tails; that is roughly 8-18% annualized on the sleeve, but capacity is limited to a few million dollars given CLF option liquidity. Panel version (20-40 verified metals meters across CLF, X, NUE, STLD, plus the aluminum secondary/rolling points): 40-80 actionable events per year, which is enough to run as a systematic operating-rate overlay rather than a series of one-off bets — realistically worth a few hundred basis points of alpha and perhaps 0.5-1.0 of incremental Sharpe on a dedicated metals equity book, before it decays. The BUS/HRC futures leg is lower-capacity and lower-frequency (maybe 1-2 clean fires a year) but has a cleaner causal claim and a real 1-3 month runway because the busheling index settles monthly off market chatter that lags physical availability. Costs are near zero: this is free government-mandated data and a nightly scraper.
- **Confidence:** Medium. The data source is verified beyond doubt — I pulled GP103 "CLEVELAND CLIFFS" and the Alcoa/AK Steel/Smelter Service points straight out of the pipelines' own published files, and the daily posting obligation is black-letter 18 CFR 284.13(d). The mechanism (gas burn ≈ DRI tons) is close to physics. The biggest uncertainty is METER COMPLETENESS AND ATTRIBUTION, and it is non-random rather than merely noisy: many US steel and aluminum plants take gas from an INTRASTATE pipeline (Texas and Louisiana especially — which is why SDI Sinton, voestalpine Texas, and possibly Nucor's Louisiana DRI may have no FERC-posted meter at all) or from a local distribution company, both of which are outside the posting regime entirely, so the panel covers the plants it covers and cannot be made comprehensive. Sub-risks: a single named point can sit behind a header serving more than one customer; a plant with dual supply (interstate tap plus LDC backup) can shift load and mask a partial derate; point names get truncated and re-labeled without notice, so the crosswalk needs maintenance; and idiosyncratically, Cliffs has publicly explored repurposing or divesting the Toledo HBI asset, which would eventually turn the flagship meter permanently dark. Secondary risk: the CLF equity leg carries all the usual confounds (tariff headlines, M&A chatter, HRC beta) that can swamp a correct plant-level call over a 2-10 week hold, which is the argument for expressing at least part of the view in the metallics futures rather than the equity.

---
## 60. The Ripple-Fire Census

**Arena:** financial-market
**Domain:** Copper, lithium & battery metals. RULES: institutions only; sanctioned collection

- **Data source:** Continuous raw seismic waveforms from the public research arrays that blanket Chile's copper belt — used to count production blasts and cave collapses at individual mines. Specifically: FDSN network CX (IPOC / "Plate Boundary Observatory Northern Chile," operated by GFZ-Potsdam, 31 stations, continuously archived since 2006) plus network C1 (Red Sismológica Nacional, Universidad de Chile, 70+ broadband + 297 free-field strong-motion instruments). The tradeable signal is the daily ripple-fired ANFO production-shot sequence at each open pit, and the mining-induced seismicity at the block-cave undergrounds. Critically, this signal exists in NO published earthquake catalog: seismologists deliberately strip blasts out as contamination, and USGS/NEIC only catalogs mining events above M2.5-3.0, while most production shots fall below that. It lives only in raw continuous miniSEED. Verified station coordinates put the array absurdly close to the world's densest copper district: PB19 (-23.905, -69.291) is ~46 km from Escondida and ~45 km from Zaldívar; PB06 (-22.706, -69.572) is ~30 km from Spence and ~33 km from Sierra Gorda; PB15 (-23.208, -69.471) is ~26 km from Lomas Bayas and ~47 km from Centinela; PB20 (-21.619, -68.842) is ~28 km from El Abra, ~73 km from Collahuasi, ~76 km from Chuquicamata/Radomiro Tomic; PB01 (-21.043, -69.487) is ~77 km from Teck's Quebrada Blanca. IPOC exists to study the Nazca subduction zone; it happens, by accident, to be a permanent 24/7 all-weather sensor grid over ~20% of world copper mine supply.
- **How to collect it:** All free, open, no credentials, fully sanctioned public research data. (1) Real-time: SeedLink at geofon.gfz.de:18000 — GEOFON states real-time feeds are available "without special permission," 512-byte miniSEED. (2) Archive/backfill: FDSN dataselect, e.g. https://geofon.gfz.de/fdsnws/dataselect/1/query?net=CX&sta=PB19&cha=HHZ&start=...&end=... ; metadata via https://geofon.gfz.de/fdsnws/station/1/query?network=CX&level=channel&format=text (I pulled this live — returns all 31 stations, HH*/HL* at 100 Hz, BH*/BL* at 20 Hz, STS-2 broadband + FBA-EST accelerometer). C1 via the EarthScope/IRIS federated catalog (irisws-fedcatalog) and CSN's own services; near-real-time located events also at sismologia.cl. (3) Software stack: ObsPy for I/O, EQcorrscan (open source, purpose-built for matched-filter detection) for the detector. Method: hand-pick one confirmed blast waveform per pit as a template, cross-correlate continuously across the 3-5 nearest stations, threshold at ~8x MAD, and emit a per-pit daily detection count plus relative amplitude (a proxy for charge mass). Template matching is what buys per-pit attribution — regional location error is 5-10 km and can't separate Chuquicamata from Radomiro Tomic, but each pit's waveform fingerprint can. Discriminate blasts from tectonic events on near-zero depth, daylight/shift-change time clustering, near-absence on Sundays, and the millisecond-delay spectral scalloping unique to ripple firing. For the collapse leg, run a geofence + depth filter on your own real-time locations. Total data cost ~$0; one workstation handles 30 stations x 3 components at 100 Hz. ~20 years of CX archive gives a genuine backtest.
- **The bet / trade:** Two legs. LEG A (production nowcast, the slow money): market-neutral single-name pairs in Chilean-concentrated copper equities held INTO their quarterly production reports — Antofagasta plc (ANTO LN: Centinela, Antucoya, Zaldívar all inside CX), Teck Resources (TECK: Quebrada Blanca inside CX), Capstone (CS CN: Mantos Blancos ~19 km from PX06), KGHM (KGH PW: Sierra Gorda ~33 km from PB06) — direction set by the 13-week blast-tonnage index versus that quarter's implied guidance run-rate, hedged with COPX or CME HG copper to strip commodity beta. Enter 3-6 weeks before the production report, exit on the report. LEG B (disruption alarm, the fast money and the better trade): long CME HG copper futures or LME 3M copper, triggered by either (i) a geofenced shallow (<3 km) M>=3.3 event located within 5 km of the El Teniente, Chuquicamata Underground, or Andina block-cave footprints, or (ii) a >72-hour blast blackout at any top-10 pit that had been firing daily. Hold intraday to 3 days; exit into the confirming company statement or wire headline.
