Overlay Leg B with Chilean-levered equity longs (ANTO LN, COPX) when the mine is Codelco's, since Codelco is unlisted and the shock has to express itself through the price and through peers.
- **Mechanism:** Rock must be broken before it can be hauled, crushed, milled and floated. A large Chilean open pit fires 1-3 production shots per day, each 50-800 tonnes of ANFO detonated in a millisecond-delayed ripple pattern. Radiated seismic energy scales with charge mass, charge mass scales with rock volume broken, and broken volume feeds — with a stockpile lag of days to weeks — mill throughput and therefore cathode/concentrate output. So a per-pit blast count and amplitude series is a direct, daily, physical measurement of the front of the production chain. When a pit stops blasting, it is because something stopped it: a strike or a stalled collective bargaining round, a fatality and the SERNAGEOMIN investigation that follows, a pit-wall failure, an altiplano winter storm, a water curtailment, a haul-fleet shortage. The blast trace goes to zero the same day; the production report lands three to four months later. Separately, block caving is a deliberately engineered rock failure, so El Teniente, Chuquicamata Underground and Andina radiate induced seismicity continuously — and when a cave misbehaves, the failure itself IS a magnitude 3-4.5 event at essentially zero depth directly beneath the mine. That is not a forecast of a disruption, it is a measurement of the disruption, and it travels at ~6 km/s to a station 40 km away in about seven seconds. El Teniente on 31 July 2025 is the archetype: an M4.2 induced event caused the collapse, six workers died, and the world's largest underground copper mine went down — the seismometers had it instantly.
- **Who's on the other side:** Sell-side mining analysts and commodity macro desks who are structurally waiting on three slow channels: company quarterly production reports (3-4 weeks after quarter end), Cochilco/INE monthly Chilean production statistics (~5-6 weeks in arrears), and Reuters/Bloomberg disruption headlines that are themselves sourced from company press releases or union statements. Also index and ETF flows in COPX/copper futures that mechanically re-rate only after the print. They are slow for a specific and durable reason: the input is not a dataset, it is raw continuous waveform data, and turning it into blast counts requires matched-filter seismology — a skill set with essentially zero overlap with commodities research staffing. Meanwhile the people who DO have the skill are grant-funded to study the Nazca subduction zone and are professionally motivated to delete exactly this signal, since blast removal is standard catalog hygiene. And the one group that already knows — the mine operators, who run dense private microseismic arrays from Nanometrics and the Institute of Mine Seismology for rockburst safety — is contractually and legally the group that cannot tell you. The operator sees it in real time; the market waits for the press release.
- **Information lead:** Three different leads. (1) Cave collapse / major induced event: 15 minutes to 6 hours ahead of the first wire headline, and you beat published catalogs too, because running your own detector on the SeedLink stream locates the event in under a minute versus several minutes for an automatic agency solution. At El Teniente the seismic record was instantaneous; Codelco's confirmation of the halt and the subsequent force majeure came hours to days later. (2) Blast blackout at a pit: same-day, versus days-to-weeks for a stoppage that isn't material enough to force an immediate disclosure — this is the bulk of the events, since most mine interruptions never clear the materiality bar until they show up in a quarterly. (3) Production trend: 6-14 weeks ahead of quarterly production reports and 5-9 weeks ahead of Cochilco's monthly national statistics, because you are watching the first link of the chain in real time while everyone else reads the last link with a lag.
- **Why still under-exploited:** There is no product here and no dataset here — only raw miniSEED and a skill barrier, which is the kind of barrier that persists. The commercial seismic-mining vendors (Nanometrics, Institute of Mine Seismology) sell in-mine safety hardware TO operators; they sell nothing to outsiders and have no incentive to. The established copper alt-data stack all sits downstream of the pit and is cloud-, revisit- and daylight-limited: Earth-i/Marex SAVANT does satellite thermal monitoring of SMELTERS, Kpler and Vortexa track CARGOES, Orbital Insight-style vendors count STOCKPILES. Nobody sells a blast catalog for anything, anywhere. The signal is invisible in every published earthquake catalog by deliberate construction — USGS explicitly notes it historically removed mining events entirely and even now only publishes them above M2.5 (western US) / M3.0 (eastern US), thresholds that exclude most production shots. So a researcher browsing catalogs sees nothing and concludes there is nothing there. Meanwhile the underlying array is unusually favorable by pure accident: IPOC was built for subduction-zone science, the Atacama is one of the quietest, driest, hardest-rock, lowest-noise seismic environments on Earth, station-to-mine standoffs are 20-90 km, and the archive runs continuously back to 2006. The bridge between "geophysics grad student who can write an EQcorrscan matched filter" and "person with a copper book" has essentially never been built.
- **Rough +EV:** Deliberately honest, and lopsided between the legs. LEG A: ~30-35 tradeable quarterly events/year across 6-8 names. Realistically the blast index explains maybe 30-50% of the variance in the mined-tonnes surprise and considerably less of the metal surprise. Expect a ~57-60% directional hit rate against a ~4% average single-day event move, netting ~15-30 bps per event on a market-neutral pair — call it 5-9% annualized on a dedicated sleeve, with thin capacity ($20-50m per leg before impact in mid-cap miners). Modest; best run as an overlay on an existing copper book rather than standalone. LEG B: 2-5 material firings per year, but with a very high hit rate, because it is a physical detection rather than a forecast — the mine either had an M4 under it or it didn't. Worth 1-3% in copper futures and 3-8% in Chilean-levered equities per firing, with a naturally tight stop since you exit into the confirming headline within hours. Roughly 150-400 bps/yr on modest notional, with excellent capacity because HG and LME 3M copper are deep and you are trading into a liquidity event. Leg B is where most of the real money sits per unit of effort. Build cost: ~2-3 months for one seismologist plus one quant, near-zero ongoing data cost, and a ~20-year backtest available for free.
- **Confidence:** Medium overall — high on Leg B, medium-low on Leg A. The single biggest uncertainty is the transfer function from blasted rock to saleable copper. Blast count and blast amplitude measure MATERIAL MOVED, which includes waste rock (so a strip-ratio change corrupts the signal), is decoupled from mill feed by weeks of stockpile buffering, and is completely blind to head grade and recovery — and most modern copper production misses are grade and recovery misses, not tonnage misses. This dataset simply cannot see grade, which caps Leg A's ceiling. Secondary uncertainties: attribution when adjacent pits cluster inside regional location error (Chuquicamata, Radomiro Tomic and Ministro Hales sit within ~10 km of each other; template matching should resolve them but this needs empirical proof before sizing), and detection completeness at the smaller leach operations whose shots may sit near the noise floor at 70-90 km standoff. I would validate by backtesting the CX archive against known historical events with unambiguous ground truth — the 2017 Escondida strike (44 days), the QB2 ramp schedule, and El Teniente 2025 — before risking capital on Leg A. Leg B needs almost no validation: it is a geofence, a depth filter, and physics.

---
